Indus Holding · INH-DE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Indus Holding memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Indus Holding was written: 49,281 words, against 11,669 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 24,589 words · written 23 October 2026, 00:42 to 01:03 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
First set of questions: Indus Holding (INH-DE), as at 2026-10-22

Preface: what the Consumer should know about the evidence base before reading

Two checks on the files. First, the latest Earnings Review (file 06, 2026-08-12) covers the H1/Q2 2026 results published on 12 August 2026, the period ending June 2026, which matches FactSet's last reported period (06/2026); the caution in the instruction is resolved and the review can be relied on. Second, the "earlier" Earnings Review (file 07) is labelled Q1 2026 but carries evidence dated up to 22 October 2026 (the MEWESTA disposal of 5 October, NuWays' conference feedback of 8 October including its downgrade to Hold on valuation, Sandvik's Q3 2026 report of 22 October, Morgan Stanley's tungsten work of 19 October). It is in practice the freshest document in the pack and I have used it as such.

Three framing points that run through every answer. (1) Indus is a permanent-ownership German industrial holding of 43 small companies in three roughly equal segments (Engineering, Infrastructure, Materials Solutions). Its normal economics are modest: FY25 revenue EUR 1,735m, adjusted EBITA EUR 147.8m (8.5%), organic growth negative in FY23, FY24 and FY25, post-tax ROCE about 8.5%, net debt/EBITDA 2.5x, and a cash conversion cycle of 203 to 209 days. (2) 2026 is not a normal year. Chinese export controls on tungsten (February 2025) took European ammonium paratungstate (APT) from about USD 330 per MTU in early 2025 to above USD 3,100 by mid-2026. Subsidiary BETEK (about half of Materials Solutions, roughly 16% of group sales) chose to keep delivering, pre-financed inventory, passed prices through and took share from smaller competitors who withdrew. Materials Solutions' adjusted EBITA margin went from 8.1% in H1 2025 to 22.4% in H1 2026 (26.9% in Q2); NuWays attributes EUR 40 to 45m of Q2 adjusted EBITA (of EUR 81.1m) to this one-off. FY26 guidance was raised twice to revenue EUR 1.90 to 2.10bn and adjusted EBITA EUR 220 to 260m, from an original EUR 150 to 170m. (3) The cost of the windfall sits on the balance sheet: working capital rose from EUR 461.5m at year-end 2025 to EUR 627m at June 2026 (FactSet's broader definition: EUR 707m, 37.9% of trailing revenue), H1 free cash flow was EUR -36.9m, and free cash flow guidance was cut from above EUR 70m to at least break-even. Every answer below has to separate what the windfall did from what the business does.

Scores are from 1 (most negative for an investment case) to 100 (most positive), 50 neutral.

External change forces

❌ D4510 questions
C-0148C-0240C-0345C-0450C-0538C-0648C-0736C-0854C-0955C-1046

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external change forces that matter for the next 18 months are one large, policy-created, temporary tailwind (the Western tungsten scarcity premium, now past its price peak and already in guidance and consensus) sitting on top of a weak German industrial base that is only beginning a shallow volume recovery, in a rates regime hostile to small leveraged acquirers. The Consumer should build the model on three separated streams: a Materials Solutions price-and-margin unwind with a dated policy watch list (Chinese licensing, Sangdong, US defence procurement rules, APT spot against USD 1,500 per MTU, the Earnings Review's own stated break level); low-single-digit volume growth in Engineering and Infrastructure with Infrastructure's digital-network demand as the most durable line; and a working-capital release that is the main cash-flow positive of FY27. The succession-wave sourcing tailwind is structural and durable but is a long-run driver of the acquisition engine, not an 18-month earnings driver, and it is becoming more contested. The skew of external paths over twelve months is negative for reported earnings and positive for cash; the cone is unusually wide for a company of this kind because one policy decision in Beijing moves group EPS by more than a full German cycle would.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical change, and visibility

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement. End-market demand for the two-thirds of the group that is not BETEK is in an early, selective and shallow cyclical recovery from a three-year German industrial recession, with 6 to 12 months of visibility from backlogs but not the two-plus years that would let the Consumer treat it as a bankable swing. The one structural element (digital networks and grid modernisation in Infrastructure) is real and is the cleanest demand story in the group. The one apparently explosive demand signal (Materials Solutions orders +44% in H1) is price, not volume, and must not be modelled as demand.

Reasoning. Three end-markets behave differently and the Consumer should model them separately.

Engineering (33% of sales) sells customised plant, automation, sensors and measurement to German and export mechanical-engineering customers. Its macro is still contracting: German mechanical-engineering real production fell 2.6% in 2026 to date, the VDMA cut its 2026 growth forecast from +1% to 0%, and capacity utilisation sits near 79% against a historical 85%, which suppresses short-cycle reorders. Against that, the segment's own indicators are better than the macro: order intake rose 3% in H1 and 10.3% in Q2, the backlog is a record EUR 430m (up 19.8% since December), and the book-to-bill is about 1.2x. The important nuance from the Q1 review is that the backlog is being carried by multi-year plant-engineering projects (largely glovebox systems and automated vehicle-assembly lines for US customers) booked in 2025 (FY25 order intake +26.9%) and scheduled for delivery in 2027 and 2028. So Engineering has revenue visibility into 2027 from a few large projects, while fresh short-cycle demand remains weak and selective. That is visibility of revenue, not of margin: the segment's H1 adjusted EBITA margin was 5.6% against a full-year target of 8.5 to 10.5%.

Infrastructure (34%) is split. Residential construction remains deeply depressed (German main-construction real revenue -2.1% in May 2026; European construction in contraction for a 53rd consecutive month; building permits up 15% but DIW forecasting zero construction investment growth), while public and telecom-driven demand for fibre entry systems, grid sealing hardware and power-distribution components is strong: segment order intake rose 23.3% in H1 to EUR 352m and the backlog is at a two-year high. The prospective EUR 500bn German infrastructure fund has not yet converted into orders (ZDB data cited by management) and is a 2027 story, not an H2 2026 story.

Materials Solutions (32%) is the distorting segment. Orders rose 44% in H1 and 61% in Q2, but management says about two-thirds of the segment's growth is price and one-third volume, and the volume element partly reflects BETEK absorbing demand that smaller competitors could no longer serve. Underlying end-demand in BETEK's markets (road milling, mining, agriculture) is flat to weak: VDMA agricultural machinery is forecast to grow 7% but Tornum at Volati and Kepler Weber show agricultural capex collapsing, and management has itself warned that high prices and renewed Chinese competition could dampen customers' willingness to pay. Non-tungsten units (medical, orthopaedics, micro-optics) grow at about 3%.

The macro has turned at the margin: the Eurozone manufacturing PMI reached 52.9 in September 2026 (the highest since May 2022) and Germany 53.9, and the sector digest judges the short-cycle destock to have ended in H1 2026. But rates are rising again (the regime in the Fitness for Fighting digest is a discount-rate shock with the ECB priced for further hikes), which is the single variable most likely to abort a construction and capex recovery before it reaches Indus's customers.

What this means for the model. Organic volume growth of low single digits in Engineering and Infrastructure for the next 12 months is the defensible base; a German capex recovery broad enough to lift Engineering margins toward target needs a rate path the current regime does not offer. Treat the EUR 500bn fund as 2027 upside, not base. Treat Materials Solutions order intake as a price series.

C-02

Competitive intensity: extent and direction of change

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement. Competitive intensity fell sharply and temporarily in BETEK's market in 2025 to 2026, is rising again from here, and has been rising steadily in the market that matters for the long-run case, the sourcing of German succession deals. The net direction over the next six months is adverse, after an unusually favourable twelve months.

Reasoning. In carbide wear tools the shortage did the competitors' retreating for BETEK: NuWays and management both report smaller European toolmakers quietly withdrawing because they could not finance or source feedstock, and some exiting permanently. That is why BETEK could raise prices repeatedly without volume discounts and still gain share. The reversal mechanism is specific and dated. Management has itself warned that Chinese suppliers are re-entering at high prices; Morgan Stanley expects non-Chinese tungsten supply to grow from 2027 (Almonty's Sangdong mine, Mitsubishi Materials nearly doubling APT capacity at Akita by 2029 and expanding European scrap processing at H.C. Starck); Sandvik sources powder from its own mine and recycling and runs a price-cost-neutral policy precisely because excessive price rises make customers switch; Kennametal expects its tungsten and pricing benefit to be heavily front-loaded, falling from USD 2.25 per share in one quarter to below USD 1 the next. The large, integrated rivals are therefore positioned to take back share as availability normalises, and the small rivals can re-enter when margins justify it (the tooling industry has several hundred fabricators, per Kennametal's annual report). BETEK's advantage was availability, which is the least durable form of advantage.

In Engineering, pricing pressure is explicit: NuWays modelled a 1.5 percentage point margin decline in Q2 on weak mechanical-engineering demand and price pressure, and the segment delivered a 5.6% H1 margin despite record orders.

In deal sourcing, the competitive set is broadening: Sdiptech names Germany as its next hunting ground with SEK 2.4bn of available funds; Lifco runs dedicated local DACH acquisition managers; family offices are cited by over 80% of private-equity managers as intense competitors; private equity pays 8 to 12x. Indus still wins on its non-exit promise at 5.5 to 6.5x EV/EBIT, but the pool of patient-capital bidders is growing and the acquired-EBITA delivery has been thin (EUR 1.9m against an EUR 8 to 9m target in the period the geography digest cites).

C-03

Disruption force: strengthening or disproved, and direction on the company

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement. The only disruption that has materially acted on Indus in the last eighteen months is a political one (China's tungsten export controls), and it acted in the company's favour through its P&L and against it through its balance sheet. Technological disruption (AI, automation, low-cost Asian tooling) is a slow, two-sided force that is neither strengthening fast enough to help nor being disproved; its net direction on a decentralised holding of small engineering firms is mildly negative because the group's defences are small relative to the exposure.

Reasoning. The tungsten shock is better treated under C-04 and C-05; here the point is that it is the only disruption force that has moved earnings, and its sign flips when it unwinds. On technology, the evidence is of competent but small-scale adoption: six AI sprints, three innovation sprints, 14 Innovation Development Bank projects (EUR 3.3m funded in 2025, about 2% of adjusted EBITA), WIRUS's 20% sales-force productivity gain, OBUK's knowledge platform, cobots at Materials subsidiaries, Gestalt Automation acquired for AI-based perception. None of this is disclosed as revenue or margin. The Q1 review's own arithmetic is the right frame: a 0.5 point margin uplift across the portfolio is worth about EUR 8.7m of EBITA, a 1 point loss from digital disintermediation about EUR 17.4m, so the downside from disruption exceeds the disclosed upside. The company's own annual report warns that AI is creating global players able to displace incumbents and that digital entrants can capture margin without owning assets. The Wisdom Library entry "AI claims are operating basics, not moats" applies directly: substitute "cheap and fast prediction" for AI in Indus's materials and the claims read as table stakes. Low-cost Chinese tooling is the more concrete threat and is addressed under C-35.

C-04

Political or regulatory change, and how it bears on the company

C50

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement. Political and regulatory change is the dominant external driver of Indus's 2026 numbers and the dominant external risk to its 2027 numbers, through one channel: Chinese critical-mineral export controls. Everything else (US tariffs, EU compliance, German fiscal stimulus, US defence procurement rules) is second-order and roughly offsetting. The Consumer should model the tungsten regime as a policy variable with a dated set of signposts, not as a commodity cycle.

Reasoning. The export controls of February 2025 restricted Chinese primary tungsten exports; China produces about 80% of global mine output. Western APT went from about USD 330 per MTU to above USD 3,100 (August 2026) while Chinese domestic prices moderated to about USD 1,700, a divergence that is itself a policy artefact. The policy signposts that keep Western prices high are: continued Chinese licensing restrictions; the US rule from 1 January 2027 restricting defence procurement of tungsten from China, Russia, Iran and North Korea (supportive of Western prices and traceable Western supply); S&P's view that new ex-China mine capacity will not close the 21,000-tonne gap before late 2028; Guotai Junan expecting high tungsten price centres through 2027. The signposts that would collapse them are: a lifting or loosening of Chinese licensing (the Change Forces report's "if lifted tomorrow, group EPS below EUR 2.00"), faster Western supply (Sangdong by 2027), and demand destruction at the current price. Western spot has already softened by about 15% from its peak (Deutsche Bank, in the Sandvik digest, mid-2026). The EU's proposed critical-raw-materials centre, stockpiling and a possible EUR 2bn fund are 2028 to 2034 items and do not help in the forecast window.

US tariffs (10% base, 25% on engineering components, up to 50% on specific metal fabrications under Section 232) hit several Materials Solutions companies in 2025 and are a standing headwind to direct exports; Indus's answer, the Grand Rapids shared facility and the METFAB and SUNBELT acquisitions, converts the headwind into a cost of localisation rather than removing it. EU compliance (CSRD across 44 portfolio groups and 180 entities, LkSG screening of 33,000 suppliers, PPWR from August 2026, CBAM moving to downstream machinery from 2028, REACH) is a recurring administrative cost that falls disproportionately on small subsidiaries. German wage regulation (collective wage growth near 3% in 2026 and 2027, minimum wage +8.4% in 2026 and +5% in 2027) is an immediate margin pressure on labour-intensive Infrastructure units. The prospective EUR 500bn German infrastructure fund and carbon contracts for difference are demand positives that the company itself says have not reached order books and will mainly affect 2027. EU foreign-investment screening (Regulation 2026/1386) lengthens cross-border M&A timetables for the EUR 500m programme.

Net. For the next six months political change is a net tailwind to reported profit and a net headwind to cash; for months six to eighteen the balance tips as the tungsten premium erodes. The asymmetry matters: the upside case (controls persist) is already in guidance and consensus; the downside case (controls ease) is not modelled by any broker.

C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: headwind or tailwind

❌ D38

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement. The tungsten input shock has been a P&L tailwind and a cash headwind simultaneously, and the two now swap signs: from H2 2026 the P&L tailwind fades as high-cost inventory flows through at a time when selling prices stop rising (management has said as much), while the cash headwind should reverse into a release as inventory is sold down. Outside tungsten, input inflation is contained in materials but rising in labour and energy. The net effect over the next six months is a headwind to margin and a tailwind to cash; over the last twelve it was the opposite.

Reasoning. The mechanics are a pass-through lag running in reverse. On the way up, BETEK bought feedstock at rising prices and sold tools at prices that rose faster and sooner (management: long procurement and processing times "brought the earnings effect forward" into Q1; contract renegotiation was at short intervals with large system partners accepting rapid adjustments to secure supply). On the way down, or even on a plateau, BETEK processes inventory bought near peak while competitors regain supply and customers push back. Management's own language is that "high material costs and delayed cost pass-through timing are projected to weigh on Materials Solutions margins during the second half" and that an abrupt drop in tungsten prices would require inventory valuation allowances. The quantities are large relative to the group: inventories reached EUR 495m at March 2026, NuWays estimates EUR 48m of the Q1 working-capital rise was tungsten, and total working capital rose EUR 165.5m in H1. Sandvik's disclosure that its powder pricing lags spot by about three months is the best available estimate of the lag in the industry.

Supplier concentration was the structural weakness that transmitted the shock: Indus deliberately prohibits central purchasing, so 33,000 suppliers are managed locally and BETEK had no group buying power to lean on; it had to pre-finance rather than negotiate. Mitigation is real but partial: European framework agreements, dual sourcing, a Shanghai facility, and a patented recycling process targeting 300 tonnes of annual demand (an unquantified share of need). The materials cost ratio rose from 44.4% to 46.1% of sales in Q1 2026. Outside tungsten, steel plate was up only 1% month on month in May 2026 and 38% below 2021 peaks; but Jefferies reports Brent above USD 100, copper up about 30% year on year and freight tightening, and personnel costs were 32% of sales and rising. Wage settlements cannot be passed through in full (the SMA insolvency of 2022 is the precedent).

For the model. Materials Solutions margin should be modelled as a declining path from Q2's 26.9% toward the guided 15 to 17% full-year (which implies H2 near 9 to 12%) and then toward the 8 to 10% range the brokers treat as normal, with the timing governed by APT and inventory turnover; working capital should be modelled as releasing EUR 100m or more over 12 to 18 months if prices stabilise, which is the single largest swing in FY27 free cash flow (consensus FY27 FCF EUR 131m against FY26 EUR 32m).

C-06

Where the company's own revenue and demand cycle is, and whether it is turning

❌ D48

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement. Reported revenue is at a cyclical peak created by price; underlying volume is at an early, fragile trough-exit. The headline cycle turns down from Q4 2026 or Q1 2027 as the price effect annualises and fades; the volume cycle turns up slowly from a low base. The Consumer should model two cycles out of phase, and expect the headline to fall while the underlying improves.

Reasoning. The sequence is clear from the quarterlies: revenue growth of -1.9%, +1.2%, -1.3%, +4.9% across 2025, then +9.8% in Q1 2026 and +20.6% in Q2 2026. Q1 organic growth of 7.5% was described by the Researcher as price, not volume; H1 organic growth of 13.3% contains, in Materials Solutions, two-thirds price. Engineering's organic growth was 2.3% in Q1 and 3.0% in H1; Infrastructure's 3.9%. That is the volume cycle: positive for the first time in three years, but at a pace that is a recovery from a depressed base rather than an upswing. Backlog visibility supports continued low-single-digit volume growth into 2027 (group backlog EUR 789m, +22.5%; book-to-bill 1.14).

The headline peak is dated by the comparison base. Q2 2026 compared with Q2 2025 at EUR 434m; by Q2 2027 the comparison is EUR 524m, and if APT has fallen even to the Chinese domestic level, Materials Solutions revenue will decline year on year. Consensus already has FY27 revenue +1.4%, which is the sell-side's acknowledgement that the headline turns. The risk is that the sell-side's FY27 number is still too high if the price unwind is faster than the volume recovery.

C-07

Macro force acting on the company, sector and industry, and where it bites hardest

❌ D36

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement. The macro force is a combination of German industrial stagnation and a rising discount rate, and it bites hardest at the company level, because Indus is more German (47 to 50% of sales against a peer median of 35 to 40% single-country exposure), more construction-linked and more working-capital-intensive than the sector median, and because its share price has already re-rated on a windfall into a regime that refuses re-ratings to small acquirers.

Reasoning. At the industry level (O. Industrials and capital goods) the macro is mixed: the short-cycle destock has ended, PMIs are above 52, but the market is paying only for scarce assets and proven conversion. At the sector level (O.19 diversified industrial groups) the picture is harsher: the sector digest's read is that the conglomerate and the serial acquirer are fragile in the 2026 regime, that the market refuses re-ratings for small acquirers, that in the 2022 sell-off the compounders' median multiple halved from 30x to 15x cash operating profit when yields rose, and that Indus and Aalberts are "parked until organic growth returns". At the company level: German GDP contracted in 2023 and 2024 and management expects only 0.5 to 0.8% in 2026; German construction was down 13% as at July 2026; 28% of industrial SMEs expect further deterioration (Gesamtmetall); and the interest-rate channel works twice, on customers' capex and on Indus's own financing of EUR 666m of net debt and a EUR 627m working-capital balance. The Fitness for Fighting digest rates Fragility at Double Underweight and asks whether a record margin is being capitalised as a run rate, which is the exact question for Indus. The company has partial offsets (international sales 53%, US local-for-local, Infrastructure's public demand) but they do not change the geography of the earnings base.

C-08

A key driver at industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand

C54

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement. Two sector-level drivers are accelerating demand and both are real but modest for the group: digital network and grid investment (Infrastructure) and the Western critical-minerals scarcity premium (Materials Solutions). The first is durable and small; the second is large and temporary. Neither is a broad industrial upcycle.

Reasoning. Digital infrastructure: public telecommunications and grid modernisation drove Infrastructure order intake up 23% in H1 and a 25% order surge in Q2, led by Hauff-Technik (cable entry systems), with grid-sealing and power-distribution hardware offsetting residential weakness; Electro Trading extends this into Swedish grid distribution. This is tied to EU decarbonisation mandates and German fiscal programmes and operates independently of the business cycle; it supports the segment's 11 to 12% margin. Scarcity premium: addressed above; it accelerated reported demand by two-thirds price and one-third volume and will decelerate on the same path. A third candidate, the German fiscal package, is a 2027 driver. A fourth, the US reindustrialisation demand captured by the Grand Rapids facility and the multi-year US plant-engineering orders, is specific to a few subsidiaries and already in the backlog.

C-09

Other material external dynamics, positive or negative

C55

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement. Three dynamics outside the questions above matter. Positive and durable: the German SME succession wave, which supplies Indus's acquisition pipeline at below-auction prices. Negative and growing: Nordic serial acquirers treating Germany as their next hunting ground, which erodes the uncontested status of that pipeline. Two-sided: the valuation gap between depressed German small caps and private transaction prices, which could attract a take-private bid (a floor) but also signals that the market will not pay for the acquirer model (a ceiling).

Reasoning. The demographics are locked in: 39% of German SME owners are 60 or older, KfW estimates 125,000 SMEs seeking successors annually through 2027 and 626,000 businesses needing management transfer by 2027, 72% of family firms fail to secure a successor, and roughly 2,500 quality companies with EUR 10 to 50m of sales will seek successors annually by 2028. Indus's model (no exit, management retained, 75% of companies held over ten years) wins conservative sellers; the geography digest records 5.5 to 6.5x EV/EBIT entry against 8 to 10x in auctions. The caveat the memo flagged is that all four research families trace this to one KfW study, so the demographic is well evidenced but not independently corroborated four times. The competition from Sdiptech, Lifco and others is documented in C-02. The valuation gap: Indus trades at about 3.7x FY26e EV/EBITDA and below 6x FY26e P/E on the windfall year, while Deutsche Bank notes widening gaps between public small caps and private targets could incentivise take-private bids; but the sector digest's reading that the market pays for subtraction (break-ups) and not addition (acquisitions) is the stronger current force. The liquidity of the shares (USD 3 to 5m a day) limits who can act on either.

C-10

The sector and industry thematic drivers in turn, and the overall external change picture

❌ D46

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement. Taking the sector digest's thematic drivers in turn. (i) Break-up re-rating: not applicable to Indus, which is adding, not subtracting; a mild negative because the market is currently paying for the opposite. (ii) Serial-acquirer multiple as a bet on organic growth: a headwind until Indus shows volume-led organic growth, which it has not since FY22. (iii) Consensus models only owned businesses, so a repaired balance sheet buying again is a stream of unpriced upgrades: a latent tailwind, but Indus's balance sheet is currently absorbing working capital rather than funding acquisitions (H1 M&A outflow EUR 16.6m against a EUR 50m cash budget; deal flow "temporarily decelerated" per NuWays, October 2026). (iv) A commodity windfall inside a reset is a second, shorter window and demand destruction at a high price is the watch item: this is Indus's live situation and the window is closing. (v) Rates are the warning sign for every compounder: live and adverse. At industry level, (vi) the short-cycle turn favours cheap geared names with costs cut and newly lit acquirers: Indus is cheap on peak earnings only and its acquisition engine is paused, so it fits the pattern poorly. (vii) Scarce-box pricing closes when new capacity lands in 2027 to 2029: this is the tungsten timetable exactly.

Overall external picture. Headwinds at value-chain level: tungsten reversal mechanics, Chinese re-entry, Engineering price pressure, wage inflation. Headwinds at industry and sector level: rates, German stagnation, the market's refusal to pay for small acquirers. Tailwinds at value-chain level: digital infrastructure demand, succession-wave sourcing, BETEK's share gains and reputation. Tailwinds at industry level: PMI recovery, the end of destocking, German fiscal programmes from 2027. The balance for the last six months was strongly positive for reported profit (the windfall) and negative for cash; for the next six months it is mildly negative for profit (windfall fading, Engineering still pressured) and positive for cash (inventory release); against the last twelve to eighteen months, which were a German industrial recession with negative organic growth, the underlying picture is slightly better. The external change is therefore large, mostly already reported, and skewed to reverse.

Back to contents

Internal change forces

❌ D4913 questions
C-1150C-1255C-1360C-1452C-1550C-1656C-1758C-1848C-1942C-2055C-8635C-8745C-2148

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The internal change forces are the quiet part of this case. Leadership is unchanged and extended, the strategy is new in name and incremental in substance, guidance has been raised dramatically for a reason that is external, standards are improving slowly in Infrastructure and not in Engineering, the perimeter is being tidied rather than reshaped, the geographic moves are sensible and small, the acquisition engine is sound but paused, the balance sheet is fully employed on inventory rather than on growth or returns, and no outside actor is pushing for anything different. The one internal act of consequence, the decision to fund BETEK through the shortage, was decisive and has paid off in reported profit, but it is a one-time bet on an external variable rather than a repeatable improvement in how the group operates, and its cost was the capacity the 2030 plan needed. The Consumer should model internal change as a slow positive drift worth low single digits of revenue and tens of basis points of margin a year, should carry the 2030 targets as a ceiling rather than a base, and should expect the next internal positive of any size (the restart of acquisitions) to follow the working-capital release rather than precede it. Against the last twelve to eighteen months the direction is better and the resourcing is worse; the net is close to neutral.

C-11

Changes in chief executive, key leaders, board or other key actors, and what they signal

C50

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement. There has been no change at the top, and the absence is itself the signal: the Supervisory Board chose continuity through the tungsten period by extending CEO Dr Johannes Schmidt to 31 December 2027 and CFO Rudolf Weichert to 31 March 2028, and no outside actor has appeared to force change. The one personnel change (a new Investor Relations head) and the one governance change (a new compensation system from 1 January 2026 with share-ownership guidelines and clawbacks) are modest positives. The one governance irregularity (Carl Martin Welcker's re-election beyond the board's own 15-year tenure guideline) is a small negative. Net: stability, with a visible gap in succession planning beyond 2027 to 2028 and in insider ownership.

Reasoning. Schmidt has sat on the Management Board since January 2006 and been CEO since July 2018; Weichert has been CFO since June 2012 and Deputy Chairman since January 2024; the three segment heads (Meyer since 2017, Großmann since 2019, Degenhart since October 2023) are unchanged. The compensation reform ties long-term incentives to EBITA growth, carbon reduction and doubling EPS by 2030, and was approved by 98.74% of shareholders; but it is cash-settled and does not create ownership, and the planned group-wide employee share plan was cancelled. Insider holdings are negligible (Schmidt 6,200 shares, the whole Management Board under 0.05% of capital, no open-market purchases in twelve months). Bianca Yijin Li replaced Dafne Sanac as head of IR in 2026 without explanation. The Nomination Committee did not meet in FY25. No named successors exist for Schmidt or Weichert; seller trust in the succession market rests heavily on Schmidt's personal network, so the 2027 expiry is a live planning item for the Consumer's year-three assumptions. There is no activist, no founder family, and the largest holder (Versicherungskammer Bayern, 6.4%) is passive.

C-12

Changes to strategy or strategic priorities, and how well articulated and resourced

C55

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement. The strategy changed in 2025 from repair (PARKOUR perform: exiting automotive, consolidating five divisions into three) to growth (EMPOWERING MITTELSTAND: acquisitions, internationalisation, engineering competence), with explicit 2030 targets. The change is well articulated and the acquisition budget is sized, but in 2026 the resourcing has been diverted: the balance sheet is funding tungsten inventory rather than deals, and deal flow has slowed. The strategy is therefore clear on paper and under-executed in the one year it has been running.

Reasoning. The 2030 ambition is revenue of about EUR 3bn (from EUR 1.74bn), adjusted EBITA above EUR 330m (from EUR 148m), an 11% adjusted EBITA margin and EPS above EUR 4.50, bridged by EUR 700m of organic growth and EUR 600m of acquired revenue from about EUR 500m of acquisitions, with first-tier targets now required to bring at least EUR 4m of adjusted EBITA and the geography widened to Benelux, Italy, Spain, Poland and Scandinavia. The architecture is coherent (two-tier M&A: holding-level platforms and subsidiary-level bolt-ons; technology fields such as Advanced Flow Technologies pooling HORNGROUP and GSR; a formal willingness to sell units that miss medium-term targets, first exercised on MEWESTA in October 2026). The arithmetic is demanding: EUR 700m of organic growth over five years is about 7% a year from a base that shrank organically for three years, and EUR 600m of acquired revenue at the stated EUR 20 to 100m target size means 10 to 20 deals. Execution in 2026: PRO VIDEO (80%, about EUR 24m revenue) and AMIRA (60%, over EUR 7m), minority buyouts at DECKMA and MESUTRONIC, EUR 16.6m of cash outflow against a EUR 50m budget, and NuWays' October note that uncertainty over BETEK's working-capital needs temporarily decelerated deal flow. The strategic choice to fund BETEK's supply was deliberate and defensible, but it reveals the resourcing constraint: at 2.3x leverage with a EUR 627m working-capital balance, the company cannot run the 2030 plan and the tungsten trade at the same time.

C-13

Guidance raised, mid-term targets set or raised

C60

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement. Guidance has been raised more, and faster, than at any point in the company's recent history: the FY26 adjusted EBITA range went from EUR 150 to 170m (March) to EUR 160 to 190m (late April) to EUR 220 to 260m (29 July), a 45% rise in the midpoint within four months, and the implied margin of 11 to 13% reaches the 2030 target four years early. The mid-term targets were set in March 2025 and have not been raised. The quality of the raise is low: it is almost entirely the Materials Solutions windfall, and the same guidance cycle cut free-cash-flow guidance from above EUR 70m to break-even.

Reasoning. The decomposition is in the segment guidance: Materials Solutions moved from "moderately higher revenue, sharply lower earnings" (the original plan, which expected tariffs and Chinese export controls to hurt) to "strongly increasing revenue and income" with a 15 to 17% margin range; Engineering (8.5 to 10.5%) and Infrastructure (10 to 12%) were left broadly unchanged. Revenue guidance moved from EUR 1.80 to 1.95bn to EUR 1.90 to 2.10bn. The Consumer should note the H2 implied by guidance: adjusted EBITA of EUR 96.5 to 136.5m against EUR 123.5m delivered in H1, so the midpoint assumes H2 below H1, consistent with management's own warning that segment margins will subside. Against the history (FY24 guidance cut in July and November 2024; FY25 revenue guidance cut in May 2025; FY23 revenue missed its initial range; the legacy target of over EUR 2.0bn of revenue by FY25 missed), this is a bidirectional guidance record driven by external forces rather than a steady upgrade cycle, which the Risks report reads as evidence that management's visibility into subsidiary earnings is limited under the decentralised model.

C-14

Improving standards in execution, operations, governance or cost discipline

C52

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Standards are improving at the margin in three places that are measurable (Infrastructure margins, holding-level operational programmes, governance mechanics) and not improving in the one place that matters most to the mid-term model (Engineering margins). The improvements are incremental and consistent with a competent holding, not evidence of a step change in the standard the group holds itself to.

Reasoning. Infrastructure's adjusted EBITA margin rose from 9.1% in H1 2025 to 11.2% in H1 2026 (12.6% in Q2), attributed by management to structural cost optimisation and repositioning of a portfolio company, which NuWays and Warburg both treat as genuine rather than tungsten-related. Holding-level programmes are specific: 12 operational status checks, 6 ERP implementations, 9 efficiency projects, 63 qualified lean managers across 7 training sessions, lean administration extended to BETOMAX. Governance: clawbacks and share-ownership guidelines from 2026, 100% independent supervisory board, an expanded internal audit function, a clean PwC opinion, related-party transactions of EUR 832k. Against that: Engineering's H1 margin of 5.6% is below both the prior year (6.1%) and the 8.5 to 10.5% target despite a record backlog and Q2 recovery to 7.1%; the personnel cost ratio has risen (31.2% to 31.9% of sales, with NuWays projecting 32.5%); the cash conversion cycle lengthened from 179 to 203 days in 2025 before the tungsten build; and the decentralised model, which the Leadership report says may hinder centralised cost control, is a standing ceiling on how far standards can be pushed from the centre. The trend is positive but slow.

C-15

Reducing the perimeter through divestments, simplification or exits

C50

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement. The large perimeter reduction is done (the automotive series-supplier exit, completed in Q3 2023, which removed the loss-making Schäfer, Selzer and SMA units), and the current programme is small and tidy rather than transformative: the first disposal under the new framework (MEWESTA, about EUR 5m of sales, October 2026, price undisclosed), IMECO's closure, legal mergers (SITEK-Spikes into BETEK, Köster into PEISELER), and a stated willingness to sell 1 to 2% of group sales of non-performers. This is portfolio hygiene, not a simplification that changes predictability.

Reasoning. The perimeter remains 43 companies in three segments. The buy-hold-develop principle means capital recycling is exceptional rather than routine: over 75% of companies have been held more than ten years and about half more than twenty, which the Risks report calls an inelastic asset base that dilutes return on capital by retaining weak subsidiaries. The 2025 move to reserve the right to sell underperformers is a meaningful philosophical shift, and the MEWESTA sale shows it is live, but the size (0.3% of sales) means it has no effect on the model. A more substantive simplification lever, consolidating subsidiaries into technology fields (Advanced Flow Technologies), is about coordination rather than exit.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly

C56

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement. Indus is extending geographically in a sensible, incremental, subsidiary-led way (local-for-local manufacturing in the US, Southern Europe through AMIRA, Scandinavia through Electro Trading, Serbia through MIKROP, the UK through COMAX, the Middle East and Japan through distribution) and has widened its acquisition mandate beyond DACH. Each move is small, adjacent and defensible; none is large enough to change the group's German weighting within the forecast horizon. The risk is organisational span, not strategic logic.

Reasoning. The Grand Rapids, Michigan facility (opened April 2026) shared by AURORA, FS-BF and HAUFF-TECHNIK, with METFAB and SUNBELT acquired to deepen US manufacturing, converts tariff exposure into local production and shares overhead across three subsidiaries, which is good design. AMIRA (bio-decontamination, Italy) is a close adjacency to MBRAUN's gloveboxes serving the same pharmaceutical customers, placing it on the "close" ring of the core-versus-adjacency test. MIKROP's Serbian expansion lowers the cost base for micro-optics. International sales rose to 53% of revenue, with Germany at 47 to 50%, so the direction is right but slow. The Earnings Review's own caution is that European holding-level M&A and sub-subsidiary international hubs increase complexity for a 45-person holding; the Q1 review adds that decentralised HR leaves the holding blind to local labour issues.

C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent

C58

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement. In Materials Solutions the price increases were real in the only sense that matters for the model: margin expanded by 14 percentage points while costs tripled, so price exceeded cost inflation by a wide margin, and customers accepted it without volume discounts. In Infrastructure price increases covered concrete and metal inflation without order cancellations (real, modest). In Engineering there is no evidence of real pricing; pricing pressure compressed the margin. The group-level answer is that real pricing power has been demonstrated, concentrated in one subsidiary, under conditions of scarcity that are already easing; management is increasingly noting that customers' willingness to pay is reaching its limit rather than noting further scope.

Reasoning. The evidence of application is strong and specific: BETEK passed through a feedstock price that tripled in 2025 and tripled again in early 2026, at short intervals, with large system partners accepting rapid adjustments to secure long-term supply capacity; NuWays attributes EUR 50 to 60m of Q2 revenue and EUR 40 to 45m of Q2 EBITA to pricing and the associated volume; the segment margin went from 8.1% to 22.4% in H1 and 26.9% in Q2. The caveat on durability is management's own: the segment development is "unique and exceptional", margins will subside as delayed material cost increases flow through, and high prices with renewed Chinese competition could reduce demand and willingness to pay. The Sandvik and Kennametal read-across is that industry pricing follows spot with a lag of roughly a quarter and that pricing gains are given back as supply normalises. For the Consumer: model Materials Solutions price as a level that peaked in Q2 2026, with the 2027 question being how much of the price is retained when APT falls, not whether further increases come.

C-18

Stepping up M&A, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement. Ambition has been stepped up (EUR 500m by 2030, larger first-tier targets, wider geography) and execution has slowed (EUR 16.6m of cash outflow in H1 2026; two small deals; deal flow temporarily decelerated by working-capital uncertainty). Discipline is good and evidenced (entry multiples of 5.5 to 6.5x EV/EBIT, under-spending the budget in weak markets, unanimous board approval and monthly subsidiary reporting). Integration is by design light (management retained, no central purchasing), which preserves what is bought but forgoes synergy. The acquisition engine is therefore sound but currently idling, and the 2030 inorganic bridge of EUR 600m of revenue is behind schedule.

Reasoning. The strategic logic (buy niche-leading, profitable, owner-managed German and European industrial technology companies with EUR 20 to 100m of sales and EBIT margins above 10%, hold them permanently) is coherent and the sourcing advantage is real (C-09). The delivery record is thinner than the narrative: six acquisitions in FY25 (five add-ons, one growth), acquired EBITA of EUR 1.9m against an EUR 8 to 9m target in the period the geography digest cites, EUR 35m spent of a EUR 70m budget in late 2024, and inorganic growth of 2.1 to 2.2% of revenue in each of FY25 and H1 2026. The risk the Business Foundations report rates as the largest to the foundations is overpaying under private-equity competition as the budget scales up; the counter-evidence is that Indus has consistently walked away rather than overpay. Integration quality is hard to judge because the model does not integrate; the historical failures (Gestalt Automation's negative FY24 contribution, IMECO, the automotive units) show the model's weakness is in what it holds, not how it absorbs.

C-19

More use of the balance sheet, stepped-up shareholder returns, or management mooting either

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used more, but for inventory, not for acquisitions or returns: net financial debt rose EUR 122m in H1 2026 to EUR 666m, buybacks have stopped (no treasury purchases in H1 2026 after 2.0m shares bought since February 2024), and the dividend rose modestly to EUR 1.30 (payout 47.7%, within the 40 to 50% policy). Management is not mooting higher returns; NuWays is, on the company's behalf, if H2 cash is strong. Consensus already assumes a FY26 dividend of EUR 1.75. The correct reading is that the balance sheet is temporarily fully employed, and the next incremental use will be deleveraging through working-capital release rather than returns.

Reasoning. Leverage sits at 2.3x net debt/EBITDA on the company's definition (within the 2.0 to 2.5x policy, no holding-level covenants, floating debt fully swapped, EUR 125m of promissory notes placed in July 2025 across 3 to 10-year tranches). The equity ratio fell from 38.4% to 37.8% against a target of around 40% by year-end 2026. The company's capital-allocation policy is clear (retain at least 50% of profit; distribute up to 50%; M&A funded from cash flow and debt, no capital increase), and nothing in the H1 communication suggests a change. The 2024 to 2025 buyback was counter-cyclical and value-sensible at a low multiple; at a share price up 69% over twelve months and 60% since July, a resumption would be less compelling. The Consumer should model dividends at policy on normalised earnings (a FY26 dividend of EUR 1.75 on EUR 5.2 of windfall EPS would be a 34% payout, which is below policy and therefore plausible, but a FY27 dividend would then fall with EPS, as consensus's EUR 1.50 implies).

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces present or plausible

C55

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement. Three positives that the preceding questions do not capture. First, and most important for cash, the working-capital release: if tungsten prices stabilise, EUR 100m or more of the EUR 165m H1 build can return over 12 to 18 months, which is why consensus free cash flow goes from EUR 32m in FY26 to EUR 131m in FY27 and why the equity ratio and leverage can repair without any operating improvement. Second, BETEK's permanent share gains and strengthened customer relationships, which NuWays and the Earnings Review treat as the key non-consensus positive: if even a third of the volume gained is retained after normalisation, Materials Solutions' baseline margin sits above the old 8 to 10%. Third, the Engineering backlog's multi-year plant projects for 2027 and 2028, which give the segment a path to its 8.5 to 10.5% margin target through utilisation rather than through a German recovery. A fourth, more speculative, is the technology-field consolidation (Advanced Flow Technologies) creating cross-selling and a US sales route.

Reasoning. The working-capital point is mechanical and the most predictable part of the next 18 months; the share-gain point is credible but unquantified by anyone (management disclosed neither the percentage-point gain nor customers won), and the Q1 review is right that the Sandvik and Kennametal read-across suggests at least partial give-back; the Engineering point depends on execution at target margins, which the segment has not shown since FY24.

C-86

Negative corporate change developments present or plausible

❌ D35

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement. The negatives cluster around one mechanism: the tungsten trade has converted a conservative balance sheet into a leveraged bet on a policy variable. Specifically: (i) inventory valuation risk on EUR 495m-plus of inventory if APT falls abruptly, which management has explicitly flagged; (ii) a margin squeeze in H2 2026 as high-cost inventory meets flat or falling selling prices; (iii) the M&A engine paused at the moment the 2030 plan needed it running; (iv) Engineering margins stuck at 5.6% despite record orders; (v) a rising personnel cost ratio under German wage settlements that cannot be passed through; (vi) a very thin insider-ownership and succession-planning position for a CEO whose term ends in 2027; and (vii) the sell-side's first valuation-driven downgrade (NuWays to Hold, October 2026) after a 60% rally, which is the first crack in a unanimous Buy consensus.

Reasoning. Items (i) to (iii) are covered above and are the ones that move the model. On (iv), the Q1 review's reading is that revenue is converting before margin and that existing projects are carrying the recovery while fresh demand is selective. On (v), the Leadership report's weak-signal list includes the escalating personnel cost ratio and the cancelled employee share plan. On (vi), seller trust in succession deals relies heavily on Schmidt's network. On (vii), the dashboard shows the buy share falling 14 points in one month. None of these is a "cockroach" in the accounting sense (clean audit, no short interest, no sell ratings), but they are the paper cuts the Wisdom Library's boiling-frog entry warns accumulate.

C-87

Have key controlling actors indicated scope for change in strategy or approach

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement. There are no controlling actors. The shareholder base is fragmented (Versicherungskammer Bayern 6.4%, H.J. Selzer et al. 5.8%, Wirtgen Invest 3.9%, VolkswagenStiftung 3.1%, treasury 3.5%, free float 69%), there is no activist, no founder, no anchor industrialist with a stated agenda, and the supervisory board is 100% independent with negligible shareholdings. The only actor that could force change is a bidder, and the share price's 69% rise over twelve months has made that less likely. For the Consumer this means strategy will not be changed from outside: the 2030 plan stands or falls on management's execution, and the governance protections (change-of-control severance of up to two years' pay) mildly favour management over shareholders in any contest.

C-21

Taken together: how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes

❌ D48

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement. The positive corporate changes are moderately strong in articulation, narrow in effect and currently under-resourced. The 2030 strategy, the two-tier M&A framework, the internationalisation moves, the operational-excellence programmes and the compensation reform are all sensible and real, but in aggregate they add low single digits to revenue and a few tens of basis points to margin per year, which is the pace of a competent holding rather than a company in transformation. The one corporate action of scale in the last eighteen months, the decision to fund BETEK's supply through the shortage, was bold and has paid off handsomely in reported profit, but it consumed the financial capacity the growth strategy needed and it is an external bet rather than an internal improvement. The Consumer should therefore model internal change as a slow positive drift (Infrastructure margin, Engineering utilisation, inorganic growth of 2 to 4% a year resuming once working capital releases) and should not model a corporate-change inflection. Against the last twelve to eighteen months, the direction is better (strategy launched, disposal mechanism live, governance upgraded) but the resourcing is worse (balance sheet fully employed, M&A slowed).
