Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4516 questions
C-2454C-2548C-2650C-2755C-2848C-2940C-3150C-8438C-3250C-3352C-3450C-3544C-3654C-3748C-3842C-3938

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry structure gives Indus a durable but contested sourcing advantage as an acquirer and a portfolio of stable, modest-margin, price-taking niches as an operator; it does not give the group pricing power, scale economies or a compounding mechanism, which is why its returns on capital sit near the bottom of its peer set and why the sector precedents place it among the underperformers rather than the compounders. The last eighteen months were an anomaly in which the value chain's disruption was absorbed by competitors and converted by BETEK into the largest profit it has ever made, at the cost of carrying the industry's inventory on Indus's own balance sheet. The next eighteen months are the unwinding of that anomaly: the integrated majors, returning small players and Chinese suppliers will restore the industry's normal lagged pass-through pricing, the policy variable that created the premium can remove it at any time, and the German capex and construction recovery that would lift the other two segments is being delayed by rates. The Consumer should model the value chain as neutral-to-mildly supportive for volume, adverse for Materials Solutions price and margin from H2 2026, and should treat the supplier side as the group's structural weak point and the source of both the upside already booked and the downside still ahead.

A note on how to read this group. Indus competes in two different industries at once. As an operating group, its 43 subsidiaries compete in dozens of small industrial niches, of which only one (BETEK's carbide-tipped wear tools) is large enough and currently important enough to analyse on its own. As a holding, it competes in the market for German succession deals against other serial acquirers, private equity and family offices. I answer each question for both where both matter, and name which one drives the score.

C-24

Market share or size rank, and distance from number one or two

C54

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement. In its one material niche, BETEK is described by the Business Foundations report as the global market leader in tungsten-carbide-tipped industrial wear tools (road milling, mining, agriculture, construction), which places it at number one in a narrow niche but far smaller than the integrated tooling majors (Sandvik Machining, Kennametal) that supply the broader cemented-carbide market. The other 42 subsidiaries are "hidden champions" that hold top-three positions in micro-niches (Hauff-Technik in cable entries, MIGUA in joint systems, MBRAUN in gloveboxes, OFA in compression stockings) but no single one is large enough to move the group. As an acquirer, Indus is one of the longest-established German succession buyers (founded 1989, listed 1995) but the market is so fragmented that no acquirer has a meaningful share: the top five players in German engineering and technical markets account for about 1%.

Reasoning. The group is mid-sized: EUR 1.7bn of revenue and 9,057 employees against Mutares at EUR 6.5bn (distressed turnarounds, not comparable), Gesco at EUR 495m (comparable model, smaller), Lifco at over 250 companies (comparable model, far larger and more profitable). Rank in the niche is less valuable than it sounds because the niche is defined narrowly; the relevant comparison for pricing power is against Sandvik and Kennametal, which have mine-to-tool integration and global distribution that BETEK does not. The distance from number one in the acquirer market is not meaningful; the distance that matters is the quality gap to Lifco and Indutrade on returns (C-51).

C-25

Market concentration and steepness of share distribution

❌ D48

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement. Both of Indus's markets are extremely fragmented, and the fragmentation is structural rather than a consolidation opportunity. The German succession market has over 99% of firms classed as Mittelstand, 6,600 machine builders of which nearly 90% are SMEs, and a top five holding about 1% of share; this is a sourcing opportunity but gives no pricing or scale power. The tooling and wear-products industry contains several hundred fabricators and niche coating businesses, most buying cemented-carbide components from larger producers; Sandvik and Epiroc together control about 90% of upstream mining equipment, and Sandvik, Kennametal and Ceratizit dominate carbide powder and inserts, but the downstream tool-making tail is long and low-cost. The top-five concentration in either market is well below 30%.

Reasoning. Fragmentation cuts both ways and the Wisdom Library's "fragmentation is usually structural, not a roll-up opportunity" entry applies: regional regulation (federal state rules in Baden-Württemberg and Bavaria), local customer relationships and owner-manager cultures keep the German industrial base decentralised, which is why Indus's model does not integrate what it buys and why no acquirer has consolidated it in thirty-five years. In tooling, fragmentation means that BETEK's share gains during the shortage were taken from small players who can re-enter when supply normalises, and that pricing is ultimately set by the large integrated producers' price-cost-neutral discipline, not by BETEK.

C-26

Stability of market shares, and new entrants' share over three to five years

C50

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement. Shares in Indus's operating niches are normally stable over multi-year periods because the products are specified, certified and embedded in customer processes; the last eighteen months are the exception, with BETEK taking share from retreating small competitors in the one market where supply security temporarily outweighed price. New entrants have taken little share in the operating niches over three to five years, with one documented exception in the broader industrial space (Chinese and Indian OEMs bidding 30 to 40% below Western capital pricing on aggregates projects, per the Sandvik digest). In the acquirer market, new entrants (Nordic compounders, family offices, search funds) have taken an unquantified but rising share of the deal flow.

Reasoning. The stability of the base is evidenced by subsidiary tenure (two-thirds of OFA's workforce over ten years), by customer lock-in through technical qualification, and by the modest organic volatility outside the tungsten effect. BETEK's gain is real but unquantified by management (neither percentage points nor customers named), and both the Q1 review and the Leadership report expect partial reversal as smaller competitors return during price normalisation. The Consumer should model BETEK's retained share gain as a sensitivity rather than a base-case assumption.

C-27

Does share consolidate toward the largest or the most advantaged, and is this company among them

C55

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement. In tooling, share is consolidating during the shortage toward the supply-secure and well-financed (BETEK, Sandvik, Kennametal) rather than the largest; Indus is among the beneficiaries now, and will be among the losers of that consolidation as it partly reverses, because its advantage was financing capacity rather than vertical integration. In the succession market, share consolidates toward acquirers with permanent capital, a credible non-exit promise and a local network; Indus is among the most advantaged on those criteria but is not the best-capitalised or most profitable, so it wins the deals that conservative sellers choose on culture and loses those that go to auction.

Reasoning. The mechanism in tooling is documented: BETEK invested EUR 68.5m in strategic inventories using holding-backed liquidity to seize volume from under-financed competitors. The mechanism in sourcing is documented in the geography digest: 5.5 to 6.5x EV/EBIT without auctions because the model never exits. The caveat is that the more advantaged acquirers on returns (Lifco, Indutrade at 14 to 25% ROIC) can pay more for the same asset and still earn their hurdle, which Indus cannot at 8.5% ROCE; its advantage is therefore in the segment of sellers who are not price-maximisers.

C-28

Competitors' attitude to competition: margin-first or volume-first; benign or willing to suffer losses; do prices fall when volume falls

❌ D48

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. In carbide tooling the large competitors are margin-first and disciplined (Sandvik's price-cost-neutral policy; Kennametal competing on brand, application expertise and service rather than price), the small competitors are survival-driven (they retreated rather than price below cost during the shortage), and prices do fall when input costs fall because the industry prices off spot with a lag. In German mechanical engineering, competitors are volume-first in a downturn: pricing pressure is explicit in Engineering and capacity utilisation at 79% means rivals cut price to fill plants. In the acquirer market, competitors range from disciplined (Lifco, Lagercrantz at 5 to 6x) to undisciplined in good times (Storskogen paid up to 10x in 2021 to 2022; private equity 8 to 12x).

Reasoning. The key insight for the model is that BETEK's pricing was not won against competitors' resistance but in their absence; when they return the industry's pricing culture is a lagged pass-through of input costs in both directions, so Materials Solutions margins will follow APT down with the same quarter's lag they followed it up. The Engineering segment's experience (record backlog, falling margin) shows what the German capital-goods pricing culture does in a trough: it holds volume at the expense of margin. Indus's own behaviour as an acquirer is margin-first (walks away from overpriced deals), which is a reason its growth is slow.

C-29

Stability of that attitude over eighteen months to three years; O'Leary instability; shark-attack risk

❌ D40

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude in tooling has been unusually stable in the sense that the large players did not launch a price war into the shortage (Sandvik explicitly avoided excessive price rises), but the next phase carries a specific shark-attack risk: Chinese suppliers re-entering European markets at prices anchored to a domestic APT price (about USD 1,700 per MTU) that is roughly half the Western price, which would undercut BETEK's inventory-cost basis while it is still processing peak-priced feedstock. That is not O'Leary-style unpredictability; it is a predictable, policy-timed attack whose date is uncertain. In the acquirer market, the attitude is cyclically unstable: discipline returns in high-rate regimes (Storskogen's 7x ceiling) and dissolves in low-rate ones.

Reasoning. Management has itself named the Chinese re-entry risk. Morgan Stanley's note that Sandvik follows a price-cost-neutral strategy because customers switch if prices overshoot is the clearest statement that the integrated incumbents will follow prices down to protect share; Kennametal's front-loaded benefit profile is the clearest statement of how fast. The Western versus Chinese price gap is the measure of the attack's potential severity and should be on the Consumer's watch list alongside absolute APT.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support

C50

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. The industry structure is supportive for the holding's sourcing model (a demographically locked pipeline of sellers in a market nobody can consolidate) and neutral-to-unsupportive for the operating subsidiaries' economics (fragmented niches with stable but modest margins, price-takers on inputs, exposed to large integrated rivals where they meet them). The support for the 2026 earnings level is fragile because it rests on a temporary supply structure in one niche. The support for the long-run sourcing advantage is durable but increasingly contested.

Reasoning. The Business Foundations report rates the German succession market as a highly favourable buyer's market and the subsidiaries' niches as defensible but with high capital intensity and German cyclicality; the Risks report's bear case is that fragmentation plus decentralisation produces a portfolio of small cyclicals with no group-level economies. Both are right. For the model, the structure supports organic growth at about German GDP plus a little from niche specialisation, inorganic growth limited by capital and discipline, and margins in the 8 to 10% range through the cycle, with the current year an outlier.

C-84

What precedents, rules of thumb and evidence show about outperformance and underperformance in this sector, and whether the company fits

❌ D38

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. The sector evidence is unusually clear and Indus fits the underperformance pattern better than the outperformance pattern. Serial acquirers that have compounded share prices (Lifco, Indutrade, Addtech, Lagercrantz) share four traits: sustained positive organic growth, incremental returns on capital well above the cost of capital (Indutrade 14% median ROIC; Lifco over 100% ROCE excluding intangibles), entry multiples held at 5 to 8x with no overpaying vintage, and net debt below about 2x so the engine never stops. Those that have underperformed (Storskogen, Volati, Mutares) share the opposite: negative or price-only organic growth, ROCE around 10% or below, an overpaying vintage or a single loss-making platform, and leverage that forced a pause. The market's rule of thumb in the current regime (sector digest) is that the acquirer's multiple is its guess at how long the engine runs and organic growth is the evidence; when organic growth returns, the multiple expands, and until then the names are parked.

Indus's fit: organic growth negative in FY23, FY24 and FY25 and price-led in 2026; post-tax ROCE of about 8.5%, which Business Foundations places in the bottom 10 to 15% of European listed serial acquirers; entry multiples disciplined (a genuine match with the outperformers); leverage of 2.3 to 2.5x and currently rising for working capital (a match with the pausers). On the commodity-windfall precedent, the Kennametal and Sandvik evidence and the sector digest's own entry ("a commodity windfall inside a reset is a second, shorter window; watch demand destruction at a high price") both say the windfall reverses and the market does not capitalise it. The 2022 precedent for the regime (compounders' median multiple halving from 30x to 15x as yields rose) applies to the premium names; Indus never had the premium to lose, but its re-rating from a 24-month-forward P/E of about 6.7x (five-year median) to 8.5x (89th percentile) on peak earnings is the same vulnerability in miniature.

For the model. The sector precedent says to treat FY26 as an outlier year, to normalise Materials Solutions margins to the 8 to 10% range plus whatever share gain survives, to grow acquired revenue at the pace the balance sheet actually funds (2 to 4% a year, not the 6 to 7% the 2030 plan implies), and to expect the multiple to track organic volume growth rather than reported EPS.

C-32

Customer demand behaviour: support for the thesis, and fragility

C50

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. Customer demand supports the thesis in two places and is fragile in both. In tooling, customers prioritised delivery security over price and accepted repeated increases; management says the relationships were "permanently solidified", but has also warned that willingness to pay is reaching its limit and that pre-buying and rental substitution are visible in adjacent industrial markets (Morgan Stanley's distributor surveys). In Infrastructure, municipal and utility customers are buying digital-network and grid hardware on multi-year programmes, which is the most robust customer behaviour in the group. In Engineering, customers are committing selectively to strategically important automation projects while deferring discretionary capex, so the backlog reflects a few large decisions rather than broad appetite. Customer concentration is low throughout (no customer above 10%; top ten about 17 to 19%), which protects against idiosyncratic churn but not against a general capex freeze.

Reasoning. The fragility in tooling is the elasticity question: carbide tools are a small fraction of a road contractor's or mine's budget (which supports pass-through) but when a tool price has multiplied the customer can delay, respecify or try an alternative supplier, and the Q1 review notes Morgan Stanley's evidence of distributor momentum weakening toward the end of Q2. The Infrastructure demand is the least fragile because it is mandate-driven; the Engineering demand is fragile because it depends on a capex recovery that rates are postponing.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and fragility

C52

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement. Indus's subsidiaries mostly sell direct to industrial customers (German industrial buyers prefer direct purchase from manufacturers), which is a modest structural advantage: the group keeps the customer relationship and the pricing conversation. The exception is construction and infrastructure products, which go almost entirely through regional distributors who command substantial local pricing influence on large infrastructure bids; that is where margins are most exposed to channel power. The route to market is not a material fragility, but distributor behaviour (pre-buying ahead of price rises, then destocking; and the absence of restocking so far per Jefferies) can distort quarterly demand in both Infrastructure and tooling.

Reasoning. Decentralised, direct selling fits the hidden-champion model and explains the customer lock-in the Business Foundations report describes (integrated logistics, custom concepts embedded in customer workflows). The cost is that the group cannot aggregate channel power, and the distributor-mediated construction products carry the Infrastructure segment's exposure to residential weakness.

C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour: does it help or hurt, and how fragile is the company's position against them

C50

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement. In 2025 to 2026 competitors' behaviour helped Indus more than anything the company did internally: small toolmakers retreated, and the integrated majors chose not to chase price. From here, competitors' behaviour hurts: the majors have vertical supply (Sandvik's own mine and recycling; Mitsubishi's Akita expansion) and will follow prices down to protect share, the small players will re-enter, and Chinese suppliers are already pricing off a domestic APT price half the Western level. BETEK's position is fragile against this because its advantage was availability and financing, which the majors have structurally and the small players will regain cyclically. In Engineering, competitors are holding volume by cutting price, which hurts Indus's margin but not its order book. In Infrastructure, competitors in residential construction are suffering demand stagnation while Indus's units gained in digital networks, which helps.

C-35

New entrants: adjacent, standard, orthogonal; how much of a threat each

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement. Three entrant types, in descending order of threat over the next 18 months. (1) Standard entrants returning rather than new: Chinese carbide-tool suppliers re-entering Europe at a lower input cost, and small European toolmakers re-entering as supply normalises; this is the main threat to Materials Solutions' 2027 margin, high probability, medium magnitude. (2) Adjacent entrants into the acquirer market: Sdiptech (Germany named as next hunting ground, SEK 2.4bn available, 800-plus pipeline), Lifco's local DACH managers, Momentum Group, family offices and search funds; medium probability of raising Indus's entry multiples or starving its pipeline of the best targets, low near-term earnings impact. (3) Orthogonal entrants: integrated Asian automation and tooling platforms selling direct to European industrial customers, Robot-as-a-Service models shifting value to software, platforms capturing margin without assets; low probability in the forecast window, potentially high magnitude beyond it. The company's own annual report names the orthogonal threat explicitly.

C-36

Substitutes or alternatives, and how that is changing

C54

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement. Direct material substitutes for tungsten carbide in wear tools are few (the Q1 review: few practical substitutes in cutting tools, armour and semiconductor applications), which underpins long-run pricing; but the behavioural substitutes (delaying replacement, running tools longer, respecifying to lower grades, switching to a competitor with available supply) are all live at today's prices, and the price multiple makes them more attractive than at any time in the past. In Engineering, the substitutes are customer insourcing of integration and software capability and more accessible flexible robotics, both slow-moving. In Infrastructure, substitution in construction is toward rental and deferral rather than away from the product. The substitute threat is rising at the margin, driven by price in tooling and by technology in engineering, and is not yet visible in volumes.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: help or hurt, and fragility

❌ D48

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement. Answered in substance at C-04. In summary: Chinese export policy helps today and is the largest fragility; US tariff policy hurts and is partly hedged by localisation; EU compliance regimes hurt by a small recurring amount; German fiscal and wage policy are opposite in sign (demand later, cost now); US defence procurement rules from 2027 help Western tungsten prices; EU critical-minerals policy is too slow to matter. The net position is a regulatory windfall whose fragility is the defining risk of the case: it can be removed by one administrative decision in Beijing on which Indus has no influence and no notice.

C-38

On whom in the value chain disruption forces act, how, and how fragile they leave the company

❌ D42

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement. The disruption forces act on suppliers first (Chinese feedstock controls restructured the upstream of a whole industry), transmitted to Indus through its deliberately decentralised, unprotected procurement (the mechanism the memo identified as the group's clearest structural weakness), and from Indus to customers through pricing. Competitors were disrupted more than Indus (small players exited). Regulators and political actors are the originators, not the recipients. The company's position is left fragile in a specific way: the group now carries the industry's disruption on its own balance sheet (EUR 627m of working capital, EUR 666m of net financial debt), so the unwinding of the disruption, which is benign for the industry, is a direct balance-sheet event for Indus.

Reasoning. This is the central insight of the Risks report's ACH and the sector digest's own reading ("a squeeze carried on the acquirer's own balance sheet unwinds through margin and inventory value when prices normalise"). The fragility is bounded (management says the inventory will convert into deliveries with customer support, Q2 free cash flow was positive EUR 37m, leverage is within policy with no covenants) but it is real and it is the item on which the Q3 report of 12 November 2026 will be read.

C-39

Suppliers' behaviour: support or source of risk

❌ D38

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement. Suppliers were the source of the largest risk event in the group's recent history and remain a source of risk, with the mitigation improving but unquantified. Chinese tungsten exporters restricted supply under state direction; BETEK responded with European framework agreements, dual sourcing, inventory and recycling; but Indus discloses no supplier concentration, contracted volumes, contract duration or recycling coverage, so the Consumer cannot verify how much of the apparent resilience is contractual and how much is spot exposure. Outside tungsten, the supplier base is diversified by design (33,000 suppliers, no joint purchasing), which limits single-point failure but forfeits scale terms; the Business Foundations report records that Indus's receivable collection (37 days) and payable terms (27 days) are both short, consistent with small subsidiaries that lack buying power.

Reasoning. The recycling programme (300 tonnes a year in the medium term) and the Shanghai facility are genuine structural improvements, and Western traceable supply is attracting a premium that could secure BETEK's feedstock from 2027. The cost of that security is the price itself: a reliable Western supply chain is a higher-cost supply chain than the pre-2025 Chinese one, so BETEK's normalised input cost is likely to settle above the old level even as its selling price falls, which is a structural, not cyclical, margin consideration.

Company - Competitive advantage

❌ D447 questions
C-4150C-4242C-4355C-4456C-4556C-4628C-4738

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Indus's competitive advantages are real, modest and local. At the subsidiary level the hidden-champion positioning holds customers and supports through-cycle margins of 8 to 10%; at BETEK a genuine quality and process advantage has been amplified for eighteen months by a scarcity advantage that is now fading; at the holding level a cultural sourcing advantage keeps the acquisition pipeline full at sensible prices but is increasingly imitated. What the group lacks is any advantage that compounds: no scale economies, no shared economies, no mechanical improvement in returns with growth, and a return on capital below its cost. For the Consumer this means the model should carry no structural margin expansion from competitive advantage, should treat BETEK's retained share and quality premium as a modest positive sensitivity on the normalised Materials Solutions margin (a few points above the old 8 to 10%, not the 15 to 17% guided for the windfall year), and should treat the sourcing advantage as what keeps inorganic growth possible at low multiples rather than as something that raises the return on that growth. The advantage profile is consistent with a company that trades at a discount to peers for good reason.

C-41

Sustainable relative advantages or moats, strength of each, widening or narrowing

C50

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement. Indus has one group-level advantage and a collection of subsidiary-level ones, and the Consumer should not confuse the two. The group-level advantage is a sourcing moat: the permanent-ownership promise wins succession deals from conservative German owners at 5.5 to 6.5x EV/EBIT without auctions. It is a cultural and reputational asset, moderate in strength, slowly narrowing as Nordic compounders and family offices copy the pitch. The subsidiary-level advantages are customer-related (technical qualification, embedded logistics, certification, long procurement cycles for specialised carbide components) and production-related at BETEK (a patented carbide recycling process, process know-how, and now a reputation for delivery security). These are moderate, niche-specific, and in BETEK's case temporarily widened by the shortage and set to narrow as supply normalises. There is no scale-based moat (decentralisation forbids it), no regulatory moat beyond product certification, and the culture is an asset for retention rather than for competition. Taken together, the moat profile is "adequate" (the Business Foundations report's own word), which is enough to hold margins in the 8 to 10% range and not enough to earn the premium returns that would make the acquirer model compound.

Reasoning. Evidence for the sourcing moat: 75% of companies held over ten years, roughly 50% over twenty, 58% over fifteen; founders' stated preference for strategic long-term owners over exit-driven buyers; entry multiples well below private equity's 8 to 12x. Evidence for subsidiary lock-in: no customer above 10%, top ten about 17%; switching friction from complex specifications in Engineering; BETEK's customers accepting rapid price adjustments to secure supply. Evidence against a group moat: ROCE of 8.5% post-tax, below the cost of capital on any reasonable estimate; the Risks report's view that returns are diluted by retaining weak subsidiaries; the Business Foundations placement in the bottom 10 to 15% of European serial acquirers on return on capital. The Wisdom Library's "six-level moat hierarchy" would place most of this at the tactics and operating-excellence levels, with BETEK's recycling and process technology as the only candidate for a structural moat, and even that is partial insulation (recycling covers an unquantified share of need).

C-42

Scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what it confers

❌ D42

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement. Indus is mid-sized as a holding and small as an operator: EUR 1.7bn of revenue across 43 companies means an average subsidiary of about EUR 40m, which is a scale at which a company has local relationships and specialist know-how but no purchasing, distribution or R&D leverage. The holding's scale (45 people in Bergisch Gladbach) confers financing capacity, which was decisive in 2026 (BETEK could pre-finance EUR 68.5m of inventory because the group could borrow), and an M&A sourcing network. It confers nothing in procurement by design. Against competitors: BETEK is a global niche leader but is dwarfed by Sandvik and Kennametal in the carbide chain; the Engineering and Infrastructure subsidiaries face larger OEMs and installers but rarely compete head-on. Against peer acquirers: Lifco has over 250 companies, Indutrade and Addtech are several times larger and more profitable; Gesco is smaller; Storskogen has 114 units and a 79-person head office. Indus's scale is enough to be a credible permanent owner and too small to be a platform.

Reasoning. The one place scale demonstrably mattered was the balance sheet in Q1 2026; the one place its absence demonstrably hurt was the same event (no central purchasing, no negotiated terms). The holding's leanness is a cost advantage (central costs are low) and a capability constraint (the Earnings Review's concern that holding-level oversight will be stretched by European and US expansion).

C-43

Realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and growth it allows over the mid term

C55

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement. For the acquirer, the addressable market is enormous relative to the company (roughly 2,500 quality companies with EUR 10 to 50m of sales seeking successors annually by 2028; 626,000 businesses needing transfer by 2027; Indus buys two to six a year), so the constraint on inorganic growth is capital and discipline, never target supply; the market allows 5 to 7% a year of acquired revenue growth if the balance sheet funds EUR 100m a year at the stated multiples, and 2 to 4% at the pace actually achieved. For the operator, the addressable markets are mature industrial niches growing with German and European GDP: DNB Carnegie forecasts German technical installation and construction at about 2% a year through 2030; NuWays expects organic growth to match domestic GDP over the cycle; the Risks report says long-term organic expansion cannot exceed real GDP. Infrastructure's digital-network and grid markets grow faster (double-digit order growth) and BETEK's end markets (road, mining, agriculture) are cyclical around a flat trend. The mid-term organic growth the market allows is therefore 2 to 4% nominal, with a cyclical recovery possibly adding to that in 2027 and the 2030 plan's EUR 700m organic bridge (about 7% a year) looking unachievable without a German boom.

C-44

Surplus customer utility against alternatives: leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior

C56

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement. For BETEK, inferiority has been disproved in the hardest possible test: customers paid multiples of the prior price, repeatedly, without volume discounts, and chose BETEK over alternatives when alternatives existed at lower prices from Chinese suppliers, because BETEK's quality (NuWays: customers accept pass-through because of product quality) and delivery reliability (customers prioritise delivery security over price) were worth more than the price difference. For the other subsidiaries, the hidden-champion positioning, long customer tenures and the absence of any evidence of share loss support "not inferior" without proving "leading". The weakest link is Engineering, where a 5.6% margin alongside record orders suggests that customers value the products but not enough to pay for them in a downturn. On the aim of disproving inferiority at this stage, the group passes, and BETEK passes strongly.

Reasoning. The caveat is that BETEK's test was run under scarcity; the Q1 review's point that the next phase may shift bargaining power back to customers as alternative supply improves means the surplus utility of delivery security will be worth less when delivery is no longer scarce. The durable component is quality and application know-how, which the Business Foundations report describes as proprietary processing technology and which third-party commentary (customers and suppliers praising supply reliability) corroborates.

C-45

Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of relative surplus utility, by application not conjecture

C56

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Judgement. Pricing power has been demonstrated by application at BETEK on a scale almost no industrial company shows, and demonstrated modestly in Infrastructure (price adjustments covering concrete and metals without cancellations). The erosion question is the open one: management has flagged that high prices threaten customers' willingness to pay in some industries, that Chinese competition is returning, and that further cost increases could pressure margins, which together say that the price-to-utility ratio has been pushed to its limit rather than that it has room left. In Engineering there is no pricing power to speak of in the current environment. Group-wide, the honest characterisation is strong, concentrated and conditional pricing power: strong where scarcity gave the subsidiary the whip hand, conditional on that scarcity lasting, and with the first signs of erosion already in management's own language.

Reasoning. The Wisdom Library's pricing-power entry asks for evidence of application, which exists here (H1 Materials Solutions revenue +32.4% with two-thirds price; margin 8.1% to 22.4%), and for evidence that utility was not eroded, which is partially contradicted by the volume gains (customers bought more, which suggests utility held) and partially supported by management's warnings (customers may buy less, which suggests it is being tested). The right modelling stance is that BETEK can hold price above the old level by a margin equal to its retained quality and supply-security premium (a few percentage points of margin), not by the scarcity premium (double-digit margin points).

C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers, stated or de facto

❌ D28

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement. No. Indus's model is the opposite of scale-economies-shared: subsidiaries are autonomous, purchasing is decentralised by policy, there is no group-wide pricing or customer programme, and the strategy, culture and values are about preserving each company's independence and entrepreneurial character, not about passing group efficiencies to customers. BETEK's 2026 behaviour (capturing the scarcity premium in full) is the explicit reverse of sharing. The only de facto sharing is at subsidiary level where a company's own learning lowers a customer's cost (for example BETEK's machine-learning tool-life simulation for jackhammer chisels), which is product value, not scale sharing. For the model this means no reinforcing loop between volume and price: growth does not lower cost per unit at group level and does not feed back into cheaper customer prices and share gains.

C-47

Gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic dynamism, improving competitive advantage and outputs against peers each period

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement. There is one gravitational force working for Indus (the demographic succession wave delivers sellers to its door each year without the company doing anything new) and it improves the opportunity set, not the financial outputs. There is no mechanical force that improves outputs with each period: the decentralised structure means acquired companies do not get cheaper to run, customers do not get stickier at group level, purchasing does not get cheaper, and the holding's central costs do not spread across a growing base in any way that shows in margins. The financial outputs against peers have, if anything, drifted the wrong way mechanically: the cash conversion cycle lengthened from 179 to 203 days in 2025 before the tungsten build, the personnel cost ratio rose, organic growth was negative three years running, and ROCE fell from 10.8% in FY23 to 8.5% in FY25. The 2026 improvement was heroic (a deliberate, courageous supply decision) rather than gravitational, and it will unwind for the same reason.

Reasoning. Compare Lifco or Indutrade, where each acquisition at 6 to 8x into a group earning 14 to 25% returns mechanically raises group earnings per share and the record of doing so compounds the sourcing reputation; Indus's acquisitions at similar multiples into a group earning 8.5% do not change the group's return profile, so the loop does not close. The Wisdom Library's "flywheel vs treadmill" entry places Indus on the treadmill: effort is required each period to stand still against German cyclicality.

Back to contents

Company - Economic strength

❌ D4011 questions
C-4940C-5050C-5134C-5236C-5346C-5434C-7544C-7652C-7736C-7850C-7934

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Indus's economic strength is that of a solid, cash-generative, modestly profitable German industrial holding: through-cycle adjusted EBITA margins of 8 to 10%, post-tax ROCE of 8 to 11%, free cash flow that normally exceeds net income because capex runs below depreciation, comfortable leverage with no covenants, and a dividend paid for over 25 years. Its weaknesses are the ones that prevent the model from compounding: working-capital intensity in the bottom tier of its peer set, a tax and interest wedge that has repeatedly stopped operating improvements reaching EPS, no EPS growth over the five-year cycle, and a wide, unpredictable perimeter. The 2026 numbers are a one-year departure from all of this created by operating leverage on an externally set price in one subsidiary, and the Consumer should build the model on the normalised base (revenue growing 2 to 4% organically plus whatever acquisitions the balance sheet funds, margins of 9 to 10% with Materials Solutions back at 8 to 12%, tax at 30 to 35%, interest of EUR 25 to 30m, PPA of EUR 21 to 25m, a flat share count), with the working-capital release as the main cash-flow positive and inventory write-downs as the main earnings risk. The fair multiple that follows is the company's own history (a 24-month-forward P/E median near 7 to 9x), not a compounder's.

C-49

EPS growth, with what revenue growth, and how durable

❌ D40

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement. The EPS record is a volatile, low-growth series with one spike. On the brokers' basis, EPS was EUR 2.29 in FY23, 2.37 in FY24 and 2.96 in FY25 (reported basic 2.06, 2.07, 2.77), on revenue that was flat to down (EUR 1,805m, 1,722m, 1,735m). Consensus has EPS of 5.21 in FY26 (+76%) on revenue +16%, then 3.94 in FY27 (-24%) on revenue +1.4%, and 4.15 in FY28 (+5%) on revenue +5.6%. Stripping the windfall, the underlying FY26 EPS is in the region of EUR 3.3 to 3.7 (Q2's EUR 1.85 less roughly EUR 1.20 of after-tax one-off, plus a normal H2), so the underlying growth from FY25's 2.96 is about 10 to 25%, driven by Infrastructure margins, Engineering volume and lower tax, with revenue growth ex-price in the low single digits. Durability: the windfall is not durable (every source agrees, including management); the underlying improvement is moderately durable if Infrastructure holds 11% and Engineering reaches its target band, and is exposed to German rates and wages. Five-year context: EPS on the Viewforth definition was EUR 3.32 in FY19 and 2.71 in FY25, so there has been no EPS growth over the cycle at all.

Reasoning. The quarterly EPS series makes the point: Q1 2026 EPS was EUR 0.64 against EUR 0.63 a year earlier despite adjusted EBITA up 70.7%, because finance costs rose (EUR -8.1m from -6.1m) and prior-year tax effects reversed; Q2 2026 EPS of EUR 1.85 is the windfall quarter. The Consumer should model the EPS bridge from EBITA with explicit lines for PPA amortisation (EUR 20.8m), net interest (about EUR 25 to 30m, rising with working-capital debt), tax at a rate that has swung between 29% and 43%, and a share count of 24.9m that is no longer shrinking.

C-50

Immediate scope to deploy capital at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and the implication for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple

C50

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement. The scope exists in principle and is constrained in practice. In principle, buying profitable niche companies at 5.5 to 6.5x EV/EBIT yields a pre-tax return on new invested capital of 15 to 18% before integration costs and PPA, which is well above the group's 8.5% blended return and above its cost of capital; EUR 100m a year of such deals for five years would add EUR 15 to 18m of EBIT a year each year, about 10% of current adjusted EBITA. In practice, the balance sheet is funding inventory, deal flow has slowed, the acquired-EBITA delivery has undershot targets, and the group's own re-investment in organic capex (EUR 60.6m in FY25 against a EUR 75 to 85m plan; consensus EUR 74 to 88m from FY26) earns a return the ROCE series suggests is modest. The mid-term potential is therefore for EPS to grow at mid-to-high single digits from a normalised base if the M&A engine restarts at full budget in 2027 and the working-capital release funds it, and for low single digits if it does not. The fair multiple implied is a low-double-digit P/E on normalised earnings (the ten-year median 24-month-forward P/E of 8.9x "prudent" band is the right anchor; the 11.4x "defendable" band would need organic growth the company has not shown), not the mid-teens multiples the Nordic compounders carry.

Reasoning. The sector digest's rule that consensus models only owned businesses, so resumed buying is a stream of unpriced upgrades for 12 to 24 months, is the upside mechanism; it requires the engine to restart, which NuWays dated to "once the situation is under control", and the Q3 report will show whether working capital has begun to release. The fair-multiple judgement follows from C-47: without a compounding mechanism, the multiple should price the current earnings power, not a growth engine.

C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax RONIC, ROCE and tangible ROCE

❌ D34

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Judgement. Low. Post-tax ROCE was about 8.5% in FY25 (NuWays), 9.2% projected for FY24, and "nine to eleven percent" through the cycle; the FY21 EBIT margin peak of 10.1% implies a cyclical high ROCE in the low teens. Return on capital employed excluding goodwill would be meaningfully higher (the portfolio was bought at low multiples, so goodwill is not the dominant drag it is at Storskogen, but acquired intangibles amortise at EUR 20.8m a year), while tangible ROCE is depressed by working-capital intensity (30 to 38% of sales) rather than by fixed assets (capex 3% of sales). Return on new invested capital from acquisitions is the one bright spot (15 to 18% pre-tax at 5.5 to 6.5x), which is why the model's returns do not improve: too little capital is deployed at the high rate and too much sits in a working-capital-heavy portfolio earning the low rate. FY26 will show a cyclical peak ROCE (adjusted EBITA EUR 220 to 260m on a capital base inflated by inventory), which the Consumer should treat as a one-year reading.

Reasoning. The comparison set is unflattering: Lifco over 100% ROCE excluding intangibles and 25%-plus including, Indutrade 14 to 20%, Addtech 18%, Sdiptech 13%, Storskogen 10%, Volati low. The Business Foundations report's placement of Indus in the bottom 10 to 15% of European serial acquirers on return on capital and the Leadership report's "moderate double-digit ROCEs around nine to eleven percent" are consistent. The cause is structural (decentralisation forgoes synergy; buy-and-hold forgoes recycling; German cyclicality depresses utilisation), so the Consumer should not model a return to the mid-teens without a change in model.

C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow, and behaviour of working capital and similar items

❌ D36

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement. Historically good conversion on top of structurally poor working capital, now temporarily broken by the tungsten build. FactSet free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) over reported net income was 174% in FY23, 135% in FY24 and 104% in FY25 (the peer table: 1.7x, 1.3x, 1.0x), helped by D&A of EUR 93m against capex of EUR 48m and by low reported net income. Consensus FY26 conversion is 0.3x (FCF EUR 32m against net income EUR 120m) because working capital absorbed EUR 165m in H1; FY27 and FY28 revert to 1.3x as it releases. The structural problem underneath is working-capital intensity: net working capital was 30 to 33% of trailing revenue through 2024 and 2025 before the build (FactSet definition), the cash conversion cycle lengthened from 179 to 203 days in 2025 (Lifco: about 80), receivable days are 37, payable days only 27, and the Business Foundations report places the group in the bottom 5% of industrial aggregators for working-capital efficiency. The behaviour of working capital is therefore the single largest driver of year-to-year free cash flow variance and the main lever for balance-sheet repair: the Consumer should model it explicitly by segment rather than as a percentage of sales.

Reasoning. The FY25 free cash flow of EUR 124m on the company's definition (against a EUR 90m target) and EUR 72m on FactSet's definition shows the definitional gap the Consumer must name; the company's definition is the one guidance uses (break-even for FY26). Lease payments (EUR 88m of lease liabilities) and acquisition outflows sit below both definitions.

C-53

Gross and operating margins, and their trend

❌ D46

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Judgement. Gross margin (FactSet) was 18.8% in FY19, 21.5% at the FY21 peak, 19.3% in FY24 and 19.5% in FY25, then 20.9% in Q1 2026 and 25.6% in Q2 2026 on tungsten pricing. Adjusted EBITA margin was 10.8% in FY23 (NuWays basis; 10.1% EBIT margin at the FY21 peak), 8.9% in FY24, 8.5% in FY25, 12.8% in H1 2026 and 15.5% in Q2 2026; guidance implies 11 to 13% for FY26 and consensus 10.7% EBIT in FY26 falling to 8.8% in FY27 and 8.7% in FY28. Segment through-cycle margins are Engineering 9.2% (FY25) falling to 5.6% (H1 2026) with a target of 8.5 to 10.5%, Infrastructure 10.4% rising to 11.2% with a target of 10 to 12%, Materials Solutions 9.4% rising to 22.4% with a windfall-year target of 15 to 17%. The trend over five years is flat-to-down at group level; the trend over the last six months is sharply up on one segment's price; the trend over the next six months is down as that price effect fades. The underlying positive trend is in Infrastructure (cost optimisation and digital-network mix), and the underlying negative one is in Engineering (utilisation and price pressure).

Reasoning. The group's normalised margin band is 8 to 10%, which the 2030 target of 11% seeks to lift through mix (acquiring companies with EBIT margins above 10%) and operational programmes; the sector median EBIT margin cited by the Change Forces report is about 7% for Engineering-type businesses and 10 to 12% for industrial peers, so Indus's normalised position is mid-pack. The Consumer should model Materials Solutions' margin path quarter by quarter from 26.9% down, since the group margin for the next four quarters is almost entirely that path.

C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base: adjusted versus reported, one-offs, capitalised costs, tax and other leakage

❌ D34

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement. The earnings base is clean in accounting terms and dirty in economic terms. Clean: PwC unqualified, no material weaknesses, no short interest, no sell ratings, related-party transactions small, over-time revenue recognition standard; the adjusted-to-reported gap is modest and consistent (adjusted EBITA EUR 147.8m against reported EBIT EUR 127.0m in FY25, a EUR 21m gap that is PPA amortisation of EUR 20.8m), and there are no capitalised development costs of note. Dirty: the FY26 earnings base contains a one-off that management itself says is not the new normal (EUR 40 to 45m of Q2 EBITA, roughly 30% of the year's guided adjusted EBITA); inventory is carried at peak prices with an explicit valuation-allowance warning; and the tax rate is a large, erratic leakage (41.8% in FY23, 43.1% in FY24, 29% in FY25 after an EUR 8.4m credit on the SMA asset sale) that has repeatedly prevented EBITA growth reaching EPS (Q1 2026: EBITA +70.7%, EPS +1.6%). Net interest (EUR 25 to 28m) is a further 15 to 20% of adjusted EBIT. The Consumer should build FY27 from a Materials Solutions margin of 8 to 12%, a tax rate of 30 to 35% (the FY25 29% is flattered by the credit; the German statutory rate plus non-deductible items and loss-making subsidiaries without tax relief has historically produced 35 to 43%), and PPA amortisation of EUR 21 to 25m rising with acquisitions.

Reasoning. The quarterly net income series (EUR 4.5m in Q4 2024, 7.7m in Q4 2025, against EUR 33.2m in Q3 2025) shows how much noise sits below EBITA; goodwill impairments (EUR 6.7m in late 2024; a 10% cut to segment EBIT plans would trigger EUR 10.5m more) and restructuring at individual subsidiaries recur. Brokers' adjusted EPS of 2.96 for FY25 is 7% above reported 2.77, a normal gap.

C-75

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how much a revenue change moves operating profit, and the range of outcomes

❌ D44

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement. Operating leverage is material and two-sided, and 2026 has shown the upside with unusual clarity. The company's own sensitivities (carried in the Change Forces report) are that a 1% change in sales moves adjusted EPS by 1.27% at constant margins, a 2% volume contraction cuts EPS by 5.26% on high fixed manufacturing costs, and a 50 basis point EBIT margin shift moves EPS by 5.26%. Materials Solutions' margin going from 8.1% to 22.4% on revenue +32% (almost all price) is operating leverage on price rather than volume: a EUR 92m revenue increase produced a EUR 61m EBITA increase, a 66% drop-through. Engineering shows the downside: a 5.6% margin on rising revenue because utilisation and price pressure dominate. The cost structure is roughly 44 to 46% materials (variable), 32% personnel (semi-fixed in Germany), and the remainder largely fixed; the Business Foundations report describes decentralised subsidiaries that must adapt production flexibly, but the group-level evidence (FY20 revenue -10.6% took the EBIT margin from 6.6% to 5.2%; FY21 +4.8% took it to 10.6%) shows high drop-through in both directions. The range of outcomes this creates over the next 18 months is wide: the Change Forces report's 18-month EPS scenarios run from EUR 2.07 (bear: rapid tungsten normalisation with inventory write-downs) through 3.63 to 4.36 (base) to 5.18 to 5.51 (bull), a 2.7x spread.

For the model. Build Materials Solutions with a high fixed-cost base and explicit price and volume lines so that the price unwind shows the same drop-through on the way down; build Engineering with utilisation as the margin driver and the 2027 to 2028 project deliveries as the utilisation driver.

C-76

Financial leverage: amplification of EPS and risk, and comfort

C52

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement. Moderate, comfortable on the company's own measures, and currently rising for the wrong reason. Net financial debt was EUR 544m at year-end 2025 (2.5x EBITDA on the company's definition; FactSet 2.0x including leases differently) and EUR 666m at June 2026 (2.3x on the company's basis because EBITDA rose; 1.97x on FactSet's trailing calculation); the policy band is 2.0 to 2.5x, there are no financial covenants at holding level, floating-rate debt is fully swapped (EUR 185m nominal of swaps at an average 2.49%, up from 1.15%), and funding is through bank lines (EUR 269m) and promissory notes (EUR 404m, plus EUR 125m placed in July 2025 across tranches to 2035). Net interest of about EUR 25 to 28m against adjusted EBIT of EUR 137 to 215m gives interest cover of 5 to 8x. The equity ratio is 37.8% against a 40% target. Amplification: with debt at about 70% of market capitalisation (EUR 666m against EUR 961m), a 10% move in enterprise value is a 17% move in equity, and the interest line absorbs about 15% of EBIT, so leverage adds meaningfully to EPS volatility without threatening solvency. The discomfort is that the debt is funding inventory whose value depends on a policy variable; if tungsten fell sharply, EBITDA would fall and write-downs would hit equity at the same time as the debt stayed, which is the one path on which 2.3x becomes 3x-plus quickly. The hedging also locks in a higher rate as the cycle turns.

C-77

How much of each major cost line is cuttable or reducible

❌ D36

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement. Little, structurally. Materials (44 to 46% of sales) are variable and fall with volume and price but are not "cuttable" by management action beyond procurement, which is decentralised by policy and therefore unleveraged. Personnel (about 32% of sales, EUR 554m, about 9,000 employees, mostly German, unionised, with wage settlements near 3% a year and a minimum wage rising 8.4% and 5%) is reducible only through productivity (lean programmes, AI tools showing 20% gains in specific sales workflows, cobots) and through closures of individual units (IMECO), and management has said wage rises cannot be passed on and must be offset by productivity; the group's history (SMA insolvency, restructuring at Gestalt, the automotive exits) shows that reducing labour costs in German subsidiaries is slow and expensive. Central costs are already minimal (45 people). Depreciation (EUR 93m) and PPA amortisation are fixed by past investment. Interest is reducible only by deleveraging, which the working-capital release will do mechanically. The realistic reducible pool over 18 months is in the low tens of millions (lean administration at BETOMAX-type projects, ERP consolidation, portfolio pruning of 1 to 2% of sales), against a cost base of EUR 1.6bn.

C-78

Beyond cost cuts, scope to improve financial outputs over the mid term; peer precedents; few big or many small; congruence with strategy

C50

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement. The scope is real, moderate and comes from a few identifiable levers rather than many small ones. (1) Working-capital normalisation: bringing the cash conversion cycle from 200-plus days toward the 150-day level the Business Foundations report cites for typical aggregators would release EUR 150m-plus over several years, which is the largest single lever and purely financial. (2) Engineering margin recovery from 5.6% to the 8.5 to 10.5% target band through delivery of the 2027 to 2028 backlog at planned utilisation: worth EUR 15 to 25m of EBITA. (3) Infrastructure holding 11 to 12% on digital-network mix and the EUR 500bn fund from 2027: worth EUR 5 to 10m. (4) Mix improvement from acquiring companies with EBIT margins above 10% and selling non-performers: slow, a few tenths of a point a year. (5) BETEK retaining part of its share gain and a quality premium: a few points on a EUR 280m-revenue subsidiary. Peer precedents show that Infrastructure-type margins of 11 to 12% are sustainable (Sdiptech's Energy & Electrification at 25% shows the ceiling for proprietary niche products) and that Engineering at 8 to 10% is normal for German niche capital goods; they do not show that a decentralised holding of this type can reach the mid-teens. All five levers are congruent with the stated strategy, and the 2030 target of 11% adjusted EBITA margin is consistent with (2) to (5) delivering together; the Consumer should regard 11% as the ceiling case for FY28 and 9 to 10% as the base.

C-79

Narrowness of the business perimeter, how it is changing, and what it means for predictability

❌ D34

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement. The perimeter is wide and widening: 43 companies (16, 14 and 13 by segment) across three segments that are themselves collections of unrelated niches (mechanical engineering, automation, media technology, cable entries, HVAC, formwork, carbide tools, orthopaedics, micro-optics), in Germany (47 to 50%), the rest of the EU (20%), North America (12%) and about a dozen other countries, with 180 consolidated entities and an acquisition mandate now spanning Western and Southern Europe. Predictability is correspondingly low at the subsidiary level and only moderately improved by diversification at the group level, because the German cycle is common to half the base and because one subsidiary (BETEK, about 16% of sales) has shown it can move group EBITA by 50% in a year. The guidance record (two cuts in 2024, one in 2025, two raises in 2026) is the practical measure of that unpredictability. The perimeter is narrowing only cosmetically (MEWESTA, IMECO) and widening materially (US, Italy, Scandinavia), so predictability will not improve within the forecast horizon. For the Consumer, the implication is to model at segment level with explicit treatment of BETEK, and to carry a wide range around any point estimate.
