Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 10,002 words · written 23 October 2026, 10:49 to 11:05 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Second set of questions: Indus Holding (INH-DE), as at 2026-10-23

Preface: what has changed since the first set, and how to read this set

Three points before the answers.

First, the evidence base. The four AlphaSense reports for this set add little that is new on the company (they draw on the same interim reports and NuWays notes the first set used) but they add three things that matter: (i) the industry's own measurement of the tungsten pass-through lags (Sandvik: one-month price lag and three-month cost lag in powder, three-month price lag and nine-month cost lag in cutting tools, per Rothschild Redburn on 13 October 2026), (ii) a dated reading of where tungsten prices now stand (Chinese domestic APT down 69% from its mid-March peak by mid-October 2026, per SMM, and a 20 October 2026 industry headline that tungsten "gave back most of its 2026 rally", while a 21 October 2026 AFP report describes a Chinese export ban on tungsten with Chinese producers pushing finished carbide tools rather than intermediates), and (iii) the structural tax detail (EUR 5.98m of FY25 tax expense from trade-tax losses stranded at loss-making partnerships that cannot be offset within the group). Points (ii) and (iii) move my numbers relative to the first set.

Second, the one place I disagree with the first set. The first set read the FY26 guidance midpoint as "modestly conservative" and expected the year to land in the upper half of the range because Western APT had softened only about 15% from its peak. The October evidence says the softening has gone much further at the Chinese domestic level, that the Western-Chinese gap has therefore widened to a level that is not sustainable for finished-tool pricing, and that the competitive attack on BETEK in 2027 comes through Chinese finished tools (which carry the domestic feedstock cost) rather than through cheaper Western feedstock. I now put FY26 at the guidance midpoint, not above it, and I put the FY27 Materials Solutions margin at 10% rather than the 11 to 12% that the first set's reading of consensus implied was the "top of the credible range". The first set also assumed consensus FY27 free cash flow of EUR 131m embedded a working-capital release of about EUR 100m; the consensus cash-flow lines in the financials block do not support that (consensus operating cash flow of EUR 206m in FY27 against consensus EBITDA of EUR 292m less tax and interest leaves no room for a release), so the cash surprise in FY27, if the release comes, is larger than the first set thought. I say more under P-09.

Third, the definitions. The company guides adjusted EBITA (before PPA amortisation and impairments). Brokers' EBITDA, the basis of FactSet consensus, is adjusted EBITA plus depreciation excluding PPA amortisation (FY25: EUR 147.8m plus about EUR 74m equals the brokers'-basis EUR 222m). Brokers' EPS is after PPA amortisation (FY25 EUR 2.96 against reported EUR 2.77, a gap of one-offs only, not PPA). FactSet's history lines (operating EBITDA EUR 230m, operating EBIT EUR 137m) sit on a third basis and I do not use them for forward comparisons. The forecast-inputs form asks for D&A excluding PPA amortisation, so the Financial Analyst's model will produce an EPS before PPA amortisation, which is about EUR 0.58 per share above the brokers' basis; I give both in the cross-check and name which is which.

Scores are from 1 (most negative effect on the forecasts for an investment case) to 100 (most positive), 50 neutral.

Financial forecasts

❌ D409 questions
P-0135P-0245P-0338P-0440P-0542P-0642P-0838P-0940P-1042

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The forecasts for Indus over the next 18 months to four years are shaped by one mechanism and its unwinding. A policy-created tungsten scarcity gave BETEK price leverage on a fixed cost base, which lifted FY26 adjusted EBITA to about EUR 238m and the margin to about 12%; the same leverage runs in reverse through Q4 2026 and H1 2027 as Chinese domestic prices have already collapsed, Western supply arrives and the pass-through mechanism that raised prices at short intervals lowers them, so FY27 adjusted EBITA falls to about EUR 174m (8.8%) and EPS to about EUR 3.2 on the brokers' basis, roughly 18% below a consensus that the two most recently updated brokers have already begun to abandon. Cost action, pricing outside BETEK and the business model's feedback loops cannot offset this, because the group has no cuttable cost of scale, no pricing power beyond cost recovery in its other segments, and no loop that compounds; what they can do is hold the through-cycle margin at 9 to 10% and let acquisitions, which restart once working capital releases, add about 3% of revenue and EUR 12 to 16m of EBITA a year from FY28, so that EPS compounds at about 9% a year from the FY25 base to about EUR 4.6 in FY30, below the 2030 targets on every line but EPS. The balance sheet moves the other way: the price unwind releases EUR 80 to 100m of working capital in FY27, free cash flow before acquisitions and dividends reaches about EUR 190m in the year earnings fall by a third, leverage stays at 2.0 to 2.2x throughout and the equity ratio reaches 40% in 2027; consensus has the earnings side of this and not the cash side. The bridge below operating profit is stable but expensive relative to FY19 to FY23 (net financial items EUR 27 to 32m, tax 33 to 34% easing a point a year from FY28, PPA rising, share count flat), which is why adjusted EBITA has to be a fifth higher than in the group's last good years to produce the same EPS. The track record says to trust the current-year guidance at its midpoint, to lean FY27 consensus down on earnings and up on cash, and to treat the 2030 plan as the ceiling of the cone. The effect of all this on the forecasts for an investment case is negative over the next 18 months (earnings fall from a level the share price has capitalised at the top of its own multiple range) and neutral-to-mildly positive beyond (a repaired balance sheet, a restarted engine and a lower tax rate on a base the market will by then have reset), with the width of the cone set by one policy variable and narrowed by two dated reports, 12 November 2026 and March 2027.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

❌ D35

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement. The operating leverage that will shape the next 18 months is not the volume leverage the company's own sensitivities describe; it is price leverage running in reverse. In FY26 a revenue increase of about EUR 270m (15.5%) carries adjusted EBITA from EUR 147.8m to about EUR 238m, a drop-through of roughly 33% on the group but about 66% inside Materials Solutions, because the incremental revenue was price with no incremental cost until the high-cost inventory arrived. In FY27 I expect group revenue to fall about 2% (EUR 40m) and adjusted EBITA to fall about 27% (EUR 64m), a drop-through of roughly 160% on the revenue decline, because the revenue that leaves is price at close to 100% margin and because EUR 17 to 20m of wage inflation lands on a semi-fixed labour base at the same time. This is the single largest fact about the FY27 forecasts and it does not respond to cost action: no amount of lean management changes the arithmetic of a price that falls faster than the cost of the inventory it was earned on. From FY28 the leverage turns mildly positive again on volume (Engineering backlog conversion, German fiscal demand reaching Infrastructure, acquisitions), but at the company's normal drop-through of 20 to 30% on low-single-digit volume growth, which adds 20 to 40 basis points of margin a year, not the points that 2026 added.

Reasoning, and how I get to the numbers. Three pieces of evidence set the shape.

(1) The cost structure makes price the lever and volume the slow variable. Materials are 43 to 46% of sales and variable; personnel is 32% of sales (EUR 554m, about 9,000 employees, mostly German and unionised, with collective wage growth near 3% in 2026 and 2027 and the minimum wage up 8.4% and 5%) and is semi-fixed; depreciation (EUR 74m excluding PPA) and holding costs (about EUR 20 to 23m) are fixed. The Operating Leverage report confirms that the H1 2026 margin expansion was price drop-through on a pre-financed low-cost inventory base, and that the adjusted materials ratio fell to 41.8% in Q1 2026 once inventory changes are taken into account, which is the signature of price rising faster than cost. The reverse is now scheduled: management's own words are that "delayed sharp rises in material costs" will weigh on H2 2026, and the industry lag structure (Sandvik's nine-month cost lag in cutting tools) means feedstock bought at USD 2,000 to 3,100 per MTU in H1 2026 is processed through H2 2026 and into H1 2027 while selling prices, which were adjusted "at short intervals" against feedstock on the way up, are adjusted the same way on the way down.

(2) The segment paths. Materials Solutions: Q1 2026 margin about 16.6%, Q2 26.9%; I model Q3 2026 at about 17.5%, Q4 at about 11% (including a modest inventory valuation allowance of around EUR 5m as Western spot follows Chinese domestic prices down), then 11%, 10%, 9.5% and 9% through the four quarters of 2027. That gives a FY26 segment margin of about 18% on revenue of about EUR 757m (above the guided 15 to 17% because H1 is already banked) and a FY27 margin of about 10% on revenue of about EUR 660m (down 13%, as roughly half the price gain is given back and about half the share gained from retreating competitors is lost to their return). The FY27 margin of 10% is above the FY24 to FY25 level of 8.8 to 9.4% because some share and a quality premium survive, and below the 11 to 12% that consensus appears to embed. Engineering: 5.6% in H1 2026, recovering to about 8.5% for FY26 on the segment's normal Q4 seasonality (H2 2025 was about 11.7% against H1 2025's 6.1%), and 9% in FY27 as the 2027 to 2028 plant-engineering backlog converts at planned utilisation; this is the one segment where positive volume leverage is visible over the horizon, worth about EUR 6m of EBITA a year. Infrastructure: 11.2% in H1 2026, about 11.3% for FY26 and FY27, with digital-network demand holding the mix and residential weakness capping it; the EUR 500bn fund is 2027 to 2028 volume at normal drop-through.

(3) The precedents for the way down. The Operating Leverage report's precedents are consistent and unflattering: Sandvik's negative operating leverage of 85% in Q1 2013 when production fell and tungsten prices fell together; Kennametal's front-loaded benefit (USD 2.25 per share in one quarter falling below USD 1 the next, per Jefferies, September 2026) and its 46.3% peak EBITDA margin against a ten-year average of 17.5%; Element Six's pre-tax profit halving in 2024 on an 11% revenue fall. Indus's own FY20 precedent (revenue -10.6%, EBIT from EUR 117.9m to EUR 25.1m) shows what volume leverage does to this group, but the FY27 case is different in kind: volume is flat to slightly up, price is falling, and the margin falls anyway. Against the last four to six years, in which the group margin moved within an 8 to 11% band on volume, the next 18 months move it from 15.5% (Q2 2026) to about 13% (Q1 2027) and back to the band, and the three years after that move it within the band on volume and mix.

What this means for the forecasts. Group adjusted EBITA margin: FY26 about 11.9%, FY27 about 8.8%, FY28 9.3%, FY29 9.5%, FY30 9.7%. The 2030 target of 11% needs Engineering at the top of its band, Materials above 10% and a step-up in acquired mix that the balance sheet has not yet funded; I treat it as the ceiling of the cone. The quarter-by-quarter path for the next 18 months is set out in the forecast inputs; the Q3 2026 Materials margin (my 17.5%) and the Q4 2026 margin (my 11%) are the first two observable tests, and a Q3 print below 14% would mean the unwind is running a quarter ahead of my path.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes EPS and the balance sheet over 18 months to four years

❌ D45

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Financial leverage is moderate, within policy, uncovenanted at holding level, and it amplifies the FY27 earnings decline by about a third more than the operating decline: with net financial items of about EUR 32m in FY26 and EUR 28m in FY27 and PPA amortisation of EUR 21 to 22m sitting between adjusted EBITA and pre-tax profit, a 27% fall in adjusted EBITA becomes a 33% fall in pre-tax profit and EPS. The balance sheet, by contrast, improves through the same period, because the working-capital release that accompanies the price unwind repays the debt that funded it: I expect net financial debt of about EUR 635m at end-2026 (2.0x my FY26 EBITDA), about EUR 570m at end-2027 (2.2x on a lower EBITDA) and about EUR 560m at end-2028 (2.0x), with the equity ratio reaching the 40% target during 2027. The interest line is a drag on EPS of about EUR 0.85 per share a year and is not the variable that moves the case; the variable is the volume of debt, which is the working-capital balance, and that is set by tungsten prices, not by treasury policy. The one path on which leverage becomes a problem rather than a drag is an abrupt tungsten collapse with inventory write-downs: EBITDA falls, equity absorbs the write-down and the debt stays until the inventory is sold, so 2.0x becomes 3x for two to four quarters; I put that at perhaps one chance in five over 18 months and it is the reason the cone is wide.

Reasoning. The structure: net financial debt EUR 665.8m at June 2026 (2.3x on the company's definition), bank lines of about EUR 269m and promissory notes of about EUR 529m including the EUR 125m placed in July 2025 across 3, 5, 7 and 10-year tranches; floating debt swapped at an average 2.49% on EUR 185m nominal; FactSet's debt figure includes EUR 88m of lease liabilities. The all-in cost is about 4.3 to 4.5% on average net debt (FY25 net interest EUR 25.3m on about EUR 580m), rising with the volume of debt rather than with rates, because the rate book is largely fixed or swapped; each further 100 basis points on the unhedged floating portion (EUR 100 to 150m) costs EUR 1 to 1.5m, which is small. Interest cover on adjusted EBITA is 7x in FY26 and 6x in FY27, comfortable. The amplification arithmetic: FY26 adjusted EBITA about EUR 238m, less PPA EUR 21.5m, less net financial items EUR 32m, gives pre-tax profit of about EUR 184m; a 10% change in EBITA is a 13% change in pre-tax profit; in FY27 (EBITA EUR 174m, pre-tax about EUR 124m) the same 10% is 14%. Equity: 37.8% equity ratio at June 2026; retained earnings of about EUR 75m in FY26 (net income EUR 121m less a EUR 1.30 dividend paid, EUR 32m, and the FY26 dividend accrues against FY27) and a shrinking balance sheet as inventory releases take it to about 40% during 2027. Goodwill of EUR 407m against equity of about EUR 790m; the disclosed sensitivity (EUR 10.5m of impairment per 10% cut to segment plans) is a small reported-earnings risk, not a solvency one.

The peer read-across says this is a mid-table balance sheet, not a fragile one: Storskogen needed two years of deleveraging to 2.0x before its engine restarted; Sdiptech came from 3.4x to 2.8x through eleven disposals; Volati sits at 4.1 to 4.5x and is muted; Gesco runs a 60% equity ratio and net debt of EUR 47m. Indus is between Gesco and Sdiptech: never needing repair, never having the capacity to run the M&A plan and a working-capital shock at once.

For the forecasts. Net financial items (interest plus other financial result, including put-option revaluations on the EUR 37.8m of minority purchase commitments, which add noise): FY26 about EUR -32m, FY27 EUR -28m, FY28 EUR -27m, FY29 EUR -27m, FY30 EUR -28m as acquisitions re-lever. Debt at about 65 to 70% of a EUR 961m market capitalisation means a 10% change in enterprise value is a 16 to 17% change in equity value, which matters more for the share price than for the forecasts. The sector digest's rule that a repaired balance sheet buying again is a stream of unpriced upgrades applies from FY28, not before.

P-03

Cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the line items, and when

❌ D38

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement. Very little, and late. The reducible pool over 18 months is in the low tens of millions against a cost base of EUR 1.75bn, it arrives in FY27 and FY28 rather than in H2 2026, and it is more than consumed by wage inflation: collective wage growth near 3% on EUR 554m of personnel cost is EUR 17m a year, the minimum-wage steps add a few million at the labour-intensive Infrastructure units, and the group's own history (the SMA insolvency of 2022, the Schäfer and Selzer exits, Gestalt's restructuring, IMECO's closure) shows that reducing German labour cost is slow, expensive and done one subsidiary at a time. The net effect of cost action on the FY27 margin is therefore about zero to minus 30 basis points (productivity offsets roughly half of wage inflation), and on FY28 to FY30 about plus 10 to 20 basis points a year as the lean, ERP and technology-field programmes compound and the disposal policy removes 1 to 2% of sales of underperformers. Cost is not a driver of the forecasts; it is a modest, lagging headwind that becomes a modest, lagging tailwind.

Reasoning. The structural evidence is decisive. (i) The decentralised model forbids the two levers that produce large savings in a holding: central purchasing (33,000 suppliers managed locally; the Operating Leverage report's Lifco expert-call evidence that centralising creates silos, against Storskogen's framework agreements for energy and raw materials that show pooling is possible without centralising) and central restructuring (HR is local, so the holding has no termination schedules to execute). (ii) The one cost line that is large and fixed, German production labour, is governed by IG Metall agreements, works-council co-determination that slows automation, and absenteeism of 15 to 19.5 days per employee a year (Bernstein); the July 2026 reform package may add flexibility over years, not quarters. (iii) The programmes that exist are real and small: 63 lean managers, 9 efficiency projects, 6 ERP implementations, 12 operational status checks, the Advanced Flow Technologies consolidation, AI sprints with a 20% productivity gain in one sales workflow; none is disclosed in euros, and the report's confidence on their euro value is "low". (iv) The one quantified precedent is IMECO: its closure removed EUR 13.7m of revenue and EUR 4.9m of wind-down expense from Materials Solutions in 2025, which is the scale of what portfolio pruning delivers (MEWESTA, sold in October 2026, is EUR 5m of revenue). (v) Peers show what is possible and that Indus is not there: Gesco's personnel ratio fell from 26.0% to 25.7% in H1 2026 through headcount discipline, Sandvik's SEK 1bn-plus of structural savings in 2019 held its 2020 margin above 17% on an 11% organic decline, Storskogen's Services margin went from 8.8% to 13.2% with half the gain from disposals; Indus's personnel ratio has gone the other way (31.2% to 31.9% in FY25, with NuWays projecting 32.5%), and in FY27 I expect it to rise again, toward 33%, because revenue falls as price unwinds while the wage bill rises.

Timing and the line items. H2 2026: no visible cost effect; the margin is a price story. FY27: wage inflation EUR 17 to 20m, productivity and pruning EUR 8 to 12m, net about EUR -8m (minus 40 basis points) inside my segment margins. FY28 to FY30: net about plus EUR 3 to 5m a year from lean, ERP, technology fields and disposals of non-performers, plus mix from acquiring companies with EBIT margins above 10%; this is the slow drift the first set described and it is in my 9.3 to 9.7% group margin path. The holding's central cost of about EUR 20 to 23m rises slightly with the four extra hires for M&A, digital and ESG.

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over 18 months to four years

❌ D40

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Price is the whole story of FY26 and the whole story of the FY27 decline, and after that it is a small positive. In FY26 Materials Solutions price adds about EUR 130m to group revenue (roughly 7.5 points of the 15.5% growth) and about 4.5 points to the group margin; Infrastructure adds about 1 to 2% of its revenue in price that covers concrete, metal and energy inflation; Engineering adds nothing and gives up a little. In FY27 Materials Solutions gives back about half of the price gain (about EUR 65m, or 3 points of group revenue growth) and the associated margin (about 3 points of group margin), Infrastructure adds 1 to 2% again, Engineering is flat; the net is price contribution of about -2.5 points to revenue growth and about -3 points to margin. From FY28 price is a normal, modest contributor: 1 to 2% a year in Infrastructure and the non-tungsten Materials units, cost-recovery in Engineering, and a retained premium at BETEK of a few points of segment margin above the pre-shock level. The decisive question, how much of BETEK's 2026 price survives, I answer at about half in FY27 and about a third from FY28, for reasons set out below; the precedents the Pricing Power report found support a range from a quarter to two-thirds, and the Q3 2026 print and the 2027 contract round (large system partners renegotiate at short intervals) will narrow it.

Reasoning. The evidence for retention: Kennametal's 2012 statement that it does not reduce pricing when tungsten falls; Sandvik's price-cost-neutral policy, which adjusts prices only when the expensive inventory has flowed through, and Morgan Stanley's view (19 October 2026) that Sandvik retains more of its price than the market expects; the US defence procurement ban on Chinese, Russian, Iranian and North Korean tungsten from 1 January 2027, which creates a traceable-supply premium; the EU's extended anti-dumping duties on Chinese tungsten carbide; the inelasticity of a tool that is about 3% of a machine shop's cost; and the customer relationships that BETEK's delivery security "permanently solidified" (management) and its share gains from smaller competitors that retreated.

The evidence against: management's own words that high prices and renewed Chinese competition could dampen demand and willingness to pay; the July 2026 expert call that large OEM customers are pushing back harder against surcharges than in the initial spike; the Chinese domestic APT price down 69% from its March peak by mid-October (SMM), which, with a Chinese export ban on intermediates still in place (AFP, 21 October 2026), means Chinese finished-tool makers now carry a feedstock cost a fraction of BETEK's and are being pushed by the state to sell finished tools abroad, so the attack comes through the product, not the powder; Almonty's Sangdong mine in commercial operation from late September 2026 and expected to supply over 40% of non-Chinese output by 2027, closing the Western gap; Mitsubishi's EUR 50m recycling expansion at H.C. Starck to 7,000 tonnes; and the fact that BETEK's prices were negotiated "at short intervals" against feedstock cost, which is a mechanism that cuts both ways. The Kennametal and Sandvik retention precedents belong to integrated producers with their own mines and recycling (Sandvik recycles 55% of its need; Ceratizit 91%); BETEK is not integrated, so the premium it can retain is the premium for quality and reliability, not the premium for scarcity.

The read-across to the other segments is clean. Infrastructure has real but modest pricing power: it passed through 2022 to 2023 inflation with a lag of several months and some volume loss (segment margin 11.4% in FY24, 10.4% in FY25), and in H1 2026 the mix shift to digital-network and grid products took the margin to 11.2% with Q2 at 12.6%; Hauff-Technik's patented sealing systems and the UK water-sector adoption show specification-driven pricing that holds, while the distributor-mediated construction products do not. Sdiptech's six-to-nine-month lag on fixed-price contracts is the benchmark for how slowly infrastructure pricing moves. Engineering has none: capacity utilisation at 77 to 79% in German mechanical engineering, competitors discounting to fill plants, customers ordering maintenance rather than upgrades (Deutsche Bank, September 2026), and Gesco's segment margin collapsing to 2.5% in H1 2025 under the same conditions; the record backlog is volume at thin price, which is why the segment's margin recovery comes from utilisation, not from price. The Nordic benchmark (Lifco at 22.4% EBITA, Indutrade at 13.8% with gross margin rising to 35.6%) shows what decentralised pricing autonomy delivers in niche products through an inflation cycle; Indus's non-tungsten subsidiaries behave like Indutrade's, at lower margins, and that is where the 1 to 2% a year comes from.

For the forecasts. Group revenue growth from price: FY26 about +8 points, FY27 about -2.5 points, FY28 to FY30 about +1 point a year. Group margin from price: FY26 about +4.5 points, FY27 about -3 points, FY28 about -0.5 points as the remaining BETEK premium erodes toward its quality floor, then neutral. The sensitivity that matters: each 10% of the 2026 BETEK price gain retained or lost in FY27 beyond my base case is about EUR 13m of revenue and about EUR 10m of adjusted EBITA, or EUR 0.27 of EPS.

P-05

Reinforcing or balancing feedback loops: how they shape the main line items over 18 months to four years

❌ D42

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Four loops are at work and they net to a balancing system, not a compounding one, over the forecast horizon. Loop one (supply security and share at BETEK) was reinforcing through 2025 and H1 2026 and is now reversing; it decides the FY27 earnings floor and I expect it to leave a permanent residue of a few points of segment margin and perhaps a third of the volume gained, not more. Loop two (working capital, leverage and the paused acquisition engine) is a balancing loop that is about to release: the same price unwind that cuts earnings in FY27 returns EUR 80 to 100m of cash, and that cash restarts acquisitions from late 2026 at a modest pace and from 2027 at the normal pace, so acquired revenue contributes about 1% in FY27 and 3% a year from FY28. Loop three (the acquirer's reputation and deal flow) is a slow reinforcing loop being slowly eroded by Nordic entrants and, at the margin, by the first disposal under the new policy; it does not change the FY27 to FY28 numbers but it caps the pace and the multiple of the 2030 programme. Loop four (buy-and-hold capping returns) is a standing balancing loop on return on capital; the willingness to divest changes it at the edges only. The net effect on the line items is that none of the four produces the self-strengthening dynamic that would lift the forecasts above the slow-drift base case, and two of them (one and two) are the mechanism that makes FY27 a year of falling earnings and rising cash at the same time.

Reasoning, loop by loop.

Loop one: supply security and share. The mechanism ran as the Feedback Loops report describes: BETEK invested EUR 68.5m in strategic inventory, kept delivering while smaller competitors could not source or finance feedstock, raised prices at short intervals with large system partners accepting the increases to secure supply, and took share; the segment margin went from 8.1% to 22.4% in H1 2026 and the order book rose 44%. The report's confidence that the loop is still operating in H2 2026 is "medium" and its own evidence says it is weakening: large OEMs are pushing back on surcharges (July 2026), Chinese domestic APT has collapsed, Sangdong is in commercial production, and Chinese producers are pushing finished tools. The precedents for what the delivering supplier keeps when supply normalises are thin but consistent: Kennametal's 2012 to 2013 and 2019 experience was margin compression once spot rolled over, with pricing held longer than cost; the 2015 Fanya collapse (APT from USD 340 to USD 200 per MTU by 2017) forced write-downs across the industry; Sandvik's integrated model retained more because its cost was its own. What reverses the loop is already in motion; what would keep it alive is a continuation of Chinese raw-material export restriction (supported by the 21 October report) together with the 2027 US defence rule, which keeps Western feedstock expensive but does nothing to stop Chinese finished tools arriving at a domestic feedstock cost. That combination is the worst configuration for a non-integrated Western toolmaker: high input cost and low-cost finished-product competition. My base case therefore gives BETEK the quality and reliability premium (segment margin about 10% in FY27 against 8.8 to 9.4% before the shock) and takes away the scarcity premium.

Loop two: working capital and the acquisition engine. The loop ran exactly as a balancing loop should: inventory up EUR 165m in H1 2026, free cash flow guidance cut from above EUR 70m to break-even, net debt up EUR 122m, M&A spend EUR 16.6m against a EUR 50m budget, deal flow "temporarily decelerated" (NuWays, 8 October 2026). The release is now the question. Three points. First, the company itself said on 5 October 2026 that it expects further acquisitions before the end of 2026, which means management already sees the working-capital pressure easing; the Feedback Loops report rates this loop "high" confidence and expects a restart in late 2026. Second, the peer evidence on release speed is two-sided: Kennametal's tungsten inventory of over USD 1bn is expected to inflect to cash only in the second half of its fiscal 2027, two to three quarters of flat prices after the peak, while Sandvik released working capital from 35.9% to 29.3% of revenue in one quarter through payables and receivables; BETEK, with long processing times and no integrated supply, is closer to Kennametal. Third, the restart threshold is the company's own policy (net debt to EBITDA below 2.5x, equity ratio toward 40%), which on my numbers is met at end-2026 (2.0x) and comfortably in 2027. So the loop releases, but the sequence is: a small restart in Q4 2026 (one or two bolt-ons), EUR 50 to 60m of deals in 2027 while working capital is still coming down, and EUR 75 to 95m a year from 2028. The precedents for how long a paused engine takes to restart (Storskogen two years, Sdiptech about eighteen months with eleven disposals, Volati still paused) are for balance sheets that were genuinely stretched; Indus's pause was a choice within policy, so its restart is faster. The line-item effect: acquired revenue about 2% in FY26 (already in H1), about 1% in FY27, about 3% a year in FY28 to FY30; acquired EBITA at 5.5 to 6.5x EV/EBIT on EUR 75 to 95m a year is EUR 12 to 16m of EBITA a year from FY28, which is the main reason my margin drifts up after FY27.

Loop three: reputation and deal flow. The reinforcing mechanism is real (referrals from sold families, regional banks and advisers; "the good guys" positioning; 5.5 to 6.5x entry multiples against 7.4x peer average and 8 to 12x private equity) and the pipeline is demographically locked (626,000 businesses needing transfer by 2027). The erosion is also real and dated: Lifco has bought 17 German companies since 2018 and three in 2026 alone (Karl Kaps, Mozelt, Hega Medical), Indutrade bought ECOROLL, PHACOTEC and Createc, Sdiptech names Germany as its next territory with SEK 2.4bn available. The MEWESTA disposal (October 2026) is a small crack in the non-exit promise; management framed it as regular evaluation of ownership status, which is the honest framing and also the one a conservative seller notices. The DNB Carnegie counter-evidence (the Nordic delegated-control pitch is less compelling to German firms that already have professional management) and the size of the market (multiple buyers can coexist) persuade me the loop is not broken, only slower. For the forecasts: entry multiples drift from 6x toward 7x over the horizon, which lowers the return on acquired capital from about 16% to about 14% pre-tax and makes the 2030 programme slightly less accretive per euro; it does not change FY27 or FY28.

Loop four: buy-and-hold capping returns. The evidence that active recycling raises returns is strong (Sdiptech's return on capital from 11.9% to 13.2% after eleven disposals at 6.7x EBIT; Storskogen's Services margin from 8.8% to 13.2% with half from disposals; Lifco and Addtech's incremental returns from active portfolio management), and Indus's 75% of companies held over ten years with a post-tax ROCE of about 8.5% is the balancing loop in action. The company has opened the door (the 2025 policy to sell units that miss medium-term targets; MEWESTA) but at 0.3% of sales a year the effect on the numbers is nil within the horizon. I carry it as a reason the margin ceiling is 10 to 11%, not higher.

Net for the line items. FY27: loop one takes about EUR 60 to 65m off adjusted EBITA, loop two returns EUR 80 to 100m of cash and restarts a small acquisition stream, loops three and four are silent. FY28 to FY30: loop two's restart adds about 3% a year of acquired revenue and EUR 12 to 16m of EBITA a year; loop three slowly raises the price paid; loop four keeps the group return in the 9 to 11% range. There is no loop in this business that converts growth into cheaper cost or stickier customers at group level, which is the reason the forecasts are a slow drift rather than a compounding path.

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and in which direction to lean

❌ D42

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement. The forecasts should be weighted by horizon and by line, not uniformly. Within the current year, the company's guidance carries high weight (it is set late and specifically, it has been met or beaten on its revised basis in each of the last three years, and the H2 2026 arithmetic is anchored by H1 actuals); lean to the midpoint on adjusted EBITA (about EUR 238m), not the upper half, because the October tungsten evidence makes the H2 Materials fade at least as fast as guidance implies. For FY27, the sell-side consensus carries low-to-moderate weight on earnings and should be leaned down (my EPS of about EUR 3.2 on the brokers' basis against a consensus median of EUR 3.94, with the two brokers who have updated since the October conference, Metzler and NuWays, already at EUR 3.63 to 3.65) and leaned up on cash (my free cash flow of about EUR 190m on my definition against a consensus FactSet-basis figure of EUR 131m that appears to embed no working-capital release). For FY28 to FY30, the company's 2030 targets carry little weight as a base and moderate weight as a ceiling; consensus FY28 (revenue +5.6%, EPS EUR 4.15) is a placeholder that assumes the acquisition engine is running at the pace it last ran in FY22, and should be leaned down on revenue and held on margin.

Reasoning. The record in the first set is the record: initial revenue guidance missed in FY23 and FY24 and the legacy EUR 2bn FY25 target missed; FY24 guidance cut twice and then met; FY25 revenue guidance cut and met, adjusted EBITA met within its cut range, free cash flow beaten by 38%; FY26 raised twice by 45% on the EBITA midpoint while free cash flow was cut to break-even; acquisition budgets under-spent in two of three years; acquired EBITA of EUR 1.9m against an EUR 8 to 9m target; organic growth negative three years running; Engineering below its margin band since FY24. The pattern is that in German downturns the company misses its initial targets and meets its revised ones, in upturns it beats, and in all environments it grows more slowly than its multi-year plans say. The 2026 beats (Q1 adjusted EBITA 65 to 72% above NuWays, Q2 116% above) came from an unforecastable external force and are not evidence of better forecasting; the same force produced the FY25 cut.

Three specific reasons to lean as I do. (i) The H2 2026 arithmetic: guidance implies H2 adjusted EBITA of EUR 96.5 to 136.5m against H1's 123.5m and H2 2025's 91.7m; my H2 of about EUR 114.5m is the midpoint and requires a Q3 Materials margin of about 17.5% and a Q4 of about 11%, which the nine-month cost lag and the collapse in Chinese domestic prices make the central path; the first set's "upside within the year" case needed Western prices to stay above USD 3,000 through Q4, which the 20 October headline that tungsten gave back most of its rally argues against. (ii) The FY27 consensus is stale in a specific way: five of the seven brokers last updated in July or August, before the October conference feedback (deal flow decelerated, BETEK "permanently solidified" relationships, tungsten "peaked for now"), and the two who have updated cut FY27 EPS to EUR 3.63 to 3.65 and FY27 EBITDA to EUR 263 to 272m against a median of EUR 292m; the median will follow them. (iii) The company never guides EPS, tax, interest or PPA, and its tax rate has swung between 29% and 43%, so the EBITA-to-EPS bridge is where broker models diverge most (FY27 EPS range EUR 3.63 to 4.99 on an EBITDA range of EUR 247 to 310m); my bridge (tax 34%, net financial items EUR 28m, PPA EUR 22m) is at the conservative end on tax and the sell-side's EUR 3.94 needs a tax rate near 30% that only the FY25 one-off credit has produced.

The lean, line by line. FY26: revenue to the midpoint (EUR 2.0bn), adjusted EBITA to the midpoint (EUR 238m), EPS below consensus (EUR 4.85 against 5.21) on tax and interest, free cash flow at the low end of "at least break-even" on the company's definition. FY27: revenue below consensus (about -2% against +1.4%), adjusted EBITA about EUR 174m against the roughly EUR 205 to 212m that consensus EBITDA and EBIT imply, EPS about EUR 3.2, free cash flow well above consensus. FY28 to FY30: revenue growth 5 to 5.5% a year (3% organic, 2 to 3% acquired) against consensus 5.6% and the 2030 plan's implied 8 to 9%; margin 9.3 to 9.7% against the 2030 target of 11%; EPS EUR 3.7 to 4.6 on the brokers' basis against the 2030 target of above EUR 4.50, which on my path is reached in FY30 only after PPA is added back.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape EPS, net income and free cash flow versus the last four to six years

❌ D38

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. The bridge from adjusted EBITA to EPS absorbs about 45% of every euro of operating profit at Indus, and over the next four years it absorbs slightly less than it did in the last four, for three reasons that partly offset: the tax rate settles at 33 to 34% rather than the 29 to 43% swings of FY23 to FY25 and then falls about a point a year from FY28 under the German corporate tax reform; net financial items step up to EUR 28 to 32m from the EUR 10 to 16m of FY19 to FY23 (a structural change made in 2024 by higher rates and higher debt, not a cyclical one); PPA amortisation rises slowly from EUR 21m to about EUR 26m as acquisitions resume; and the share count, which fell 8% from 26.9m to 24.9m through the 2024 to 2025 buyback, now stays flat, so EPS growth has to come from net income alone. Minorities are small (EUR 1 to 2.5m) but will grow with the 60 to 80% deal structures now in use, and the EUR 37.8m of put-option liabilities add revaluation noise to the financial result. One-offs are the swing item: an inventory valuation allowance of EUR 5 to 20m in Q4 2026 or H1 2027 is more likely than not on the current tungsten path (I carry EUR 5m), goodwill impairments of EUR 1 to 10m recur in most years, and restructuring at individual subsidiaries recurs. Free cash flow is shaped below EBITA by cash tax (which lags book tax by a year), interest, leases of about EUR 22 to 26m a year that sit outside both the company's and FactSet's free cash flow definitions, and capex of EUR 50 to 63m on a cash basis, which is below the EUR 74 to 88m consensus carries because consensus appears to be on the company's gross investment definition that includes right-of-use additions.

Reasoning. Tax: the EPS-bridge report resolves the volatility. The German statutory burden for a holding of limited partnerships is about 30 to 31% (corporate income tax, solidarity surcharge and trade tax), but trade tax is assessed partnership by partnership and losses at loss-making subsidiaries cannot be offset against profits elsewhere, which added EUR 5.98m to FY25 tax expense (about 6 points of rate) and more in FY23 and FY24 when more subsidiaries made losses; the FY25 rate of 29% was flattered by the EUR 8.36m credit on the SMA sale (about EUR 0.34 of EPS). H1 2026 ran at about 36% (Q1 above 40% on prior-year effects, Q2 about 32%). The structural rate when few subsidiaries lose money, as in a windfall year, is about 33 to 34%; in a German downturn it rises toward 36 to 38% as stranded losses grow; from FY28 the corporate income tax rate falls one point a year from 15% toward 10%, which takes about a point a year off the group rate, with a possible one-off non-cash deferred-tax revaluation on the way (sign unknown, probably small). I use 34% for FY26 and FY27, 33% for FY28, 32% for FY29, 31% for FY30. Gesco's 43.8% in FY24 and 31.6% in FY23 show the same structure at the closest peer.

Interest and financing: covered in P-02; EUR 32m in FY26 on the peak debt balance, EUR 28m in FY27, EUR 27 to 28m thereafter. Against FY19 to FY23 (EUR 10 to 16m), this is a permanent step-up of about EUR 15m a year, or EUR 0.40 of EPS, which is the main reason EPS on the Viewforth definition was EUR 3.3 in FY19 and EUR 2.7 in FY25 on similar operating profit.

PPA and impairments: EUR 20.8m in FY25, EUR 10.7m in H1 2026 (flat), rising with the EUR 500m programme; at the company's deal mix, each EUR 100m spent adds about EUR 3 to 4m of annual amortisation, so EUR 22m in FY27, 24m in FY28, 25m in FY29, 26m in FY30. The brokers deduct PPA in their EPS; the Financial Analyst's model, with D&A excluding PPA, will not, so the model's EPS will run about EUR 0.58 to 0.74 above the brokers' basis; I give both in the cross-check. Goodwill of EUR 407m across 44 cash-generating units, with EUR 10.5m of impairment per 10% cut to plans, is a recurring EUR 1 to 10m charge below adjusted EBITA.

Share count and distributions: 25.8m shares issued, about 24.9m outstanding after the 2024 to 2025 buyback (1.85m shares, 6.9% of capital, 1.1m retired in March 2025); no buyback in H1 2026 and none likely while the balance sheet funds inventory and the share trades at the 89th percentile of its own multiple range; I carry zero in FY26 to FY28 and a token EUR 10m a year in FY29 and FY30. Dividend: 40 to 50% of net profit; FY25 EUR 1.30 (47.9%); for FY26 the policy on my EPS of EUR 4.85 would give EUR 1.95 to 2.40, but management will treat a windfall year with a stretched balance sheet at the low end, so I carry EUR 1.90 (consensus EUR 1.75); FY27 EUR 1.50 (consensus 1.50), FY28 EUR 1.60, FY29 EUR 1.75, FY30 EUR 1.90. Dividend cash out is about EUR 32m in 2026, 47m in 2027, 37m in 2028.

Minorities and associates: minorities of EUR 1.6m on the balance sheet, rising with PRO VIDEO (80%) and AMIRA (60%) and future first-tier deals; associates negligible. Put-option commitments of EUR 37.8m at fair value revalue through other financial income, which is why "net financial items" is a better input than "net interest".

Free cash flow mechanics: cash tax follows book tax with a lag (FY26 cash tax reflects FY25 book tax plus FY26 prepayments, so cash tax in FY27 will be high relative to FY27 profit), lease payments of about EUR 22m a year on EUR 88m of liabilities, cash capex of EUR 48m in FY25 (FactSet) against the company's EUR 60.6m "investment in property and equipment", which I read as including right-of-use additions; I carry cash capex of EUR 50m, 55m, 58m, 60m, 63m and leases of EUR 22m to 26m separately.

Versus the last four to six years. EPS converted about 55% of adjusted EBITA growth into EPS growth in FY21 to FY23 and about 40% in FY24 to FY25 (tax and interest both rose); on my base case the conversion is about 52% over FY27 to FY30, because the tax rate is steadier and the share count is flat. Net income on the brokers' basis: about EUR 121m in FY26, 80m in FY27, 93m in FY28, 103m in FY29, 113m in FY30. Free cash flow before acquisitions and dividends but after leases: about EUR 6m in FY26, 190m in FY27, 120m in FY28, 114m in FY29, 125m in FY30; after dividends and acquisitions, net debt falls through FY27 and is roughly flat thereafter as the acquisition programme absorbs the surplus.

P-09

The net likely impact on the main financial line items over 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

❌ D40

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. Netting P-01 to P-08, the next four years look like this against FY21 to FY25. Revenue: FY21 to FY25 compounded at about 1.5% a year with organic growth negative in each of the last three; the next four compound at about 3.3% a year from the FY26 base (FY26 +15.5%, FY27 -2%, then +5 to 5.5%), with organic volume of 2 to 3% a year and acquisitions of 2 to 3% a year replacing the price that leaves. Adjusted EBITA margin: 8 to 10.8% over FY21 to FY25 (FY21 to FY23 at the top, FY24 to FY25 at the bottom); 11.9% in FY26, 8.8% in FY27, then 9.3 to 9.7%, which is the middle of the old band, not above it. EPS on the brokers' basis: EUR 2.3 to 3.0 in FY23 to FY25; EUR 4.85 in FY26, 3.2 in FY27, 3.7 in FY28, 4.2 in FY29, 4.6 in FY30, so the FY27 trough is about 10% above the FY25 base and the FY30 level is about 55% above it, a compound rate of about 9% from FY25 that comes one-third from margin recovery toward the middle of the band, one-third from acquisitions, and one-third from a lower tax rate and volume. Free cash flow: FY23 to FY25 averaged about EUR 96m on FactSet's definition on net income of about EUR 69m; the next four years average about EUR 110m on my after-lease definition, with FY27 at about EUR 190m and FY26 at about zero, so the cumulative is similar to history but the timing is inverted relative to earnings. Net debt: 2.0 to 2.5x throughout, with the equity ratio reaching 40% in 2027. The net picture is a company whose earnings go up and then down over the next 18 months and whose cash and balance sheet go down and then up, and which then resumes the slow drift of a competent holding: the four years after FY26 look like the four years before it with a slightly higher base, a slightly lower tax rate and a working acquisition engine.

The three things that join up and that the Consumer should hold on to.

(1) Earnings and cash move in opposite directions in FY27, and consensus has modelled the earnings side but not the cash side. On my numbers FY27 adjusted EBITA falls 27% and EPS falls 34% while free cash flow before acquisitions and dividends rises from about zero to about EUR 190m (EUR 210m on FactSet's definition before leases), because the working-capital balance falls from about EUR 680m at end-2026 (FactSet definition) to about EUR 600m at end-2027 as inventory values and receivables deflate with tungsten. Consensus FY27 free cash flow of EUR 131m, read against consensus EBITDA of EUR 292m less tax and interest, embeds roughly no release; consensus FY26 operating cash flow of EUR 102m embeds an absorption of about EUR 130m, which is right. So if the price unwind comes as I expect, the sell-side will be cutting FY27 earnings and raising FY27 cash in the same revision, and the second part is not yet in anyone's number. The caveat is that these two outcomes are linked by the same variable: if Western tungsten prices hold up, earnings are better and the release is smaller; if they collapse, earnings are worse (with write-downs) and the release is larger but partly consumed by the write-down. Within the enterprise value the two largely offset; it is the equity narrative that will swing.

(2) The margin band has not moved. Everything in this set says the through-cycle adjusted EBITA margin stays at 9 to 10%: the cost base cannot be cut (P-03), pricing power outside BETEK covers cost and no more (P-04), there is no compounding loop (P-05), and BETEK's retained premium is worth a few points of one segment, or about 50 to 80 basis points of the group. The 2030 target of 11% needs a step in mix from acquisitions that the balance sheet will fund only from 2028, and Engineering at the top of its band, which it has not reached since FY22. My FY30 margin of 9.7% is the base; 11% is the ceiling.

(3) The EPS bridge is stable but expensive, and that is new relative to FY19 to FY23. Net financial items of EUR 27 to 32m against EUR 10 to 16m before 2024, a structural tax rate of 33 to 34% against the 28 to 34% the group paid in its good years, PPA rising with the programme, and a share count that has stopped falling mean that adjusted EBITA has to be about 20% higher than in FY21 to FY23 to produce the same EPS. The brokers' FY28 EPS of EUR 4.15 needs adjusted EBITA of about EUR 210m at a 30% tax rate; my EUR 3.73 is the same EBITA at 33% less EUR 18m of EBITA.

The skew and the width of the cone over twelve months. The skew of the FY27 earnings path is negative relative to consensus and roughly symmetric around my base case: upside if Western prices hold through the 2027 contract round and BETEK retains two-thirds of its price (FY27 EPS about EUR 3.8), downside if Chinese finished tools and returning small competitors force BETEK back to its pre-shock margin with a EUR 20m write-down (FY27 EPS about EUR 2.5). The cash path is skewed positive relative to consensus on every branch except a prolonged plateau at peak prices, which is the least likely branch. The width is unusually large for a company of this size because one subsidiary's price, set by policy in Beijing, moves group EBITA by 25 to 30% between the branches; the Q3 2026 report on 12 November and the FY26 report in March 2027 are the dated events that narrow it.

P-10

The base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over 18 months to four years

❌ D42

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. The base case is a windfall year, a normalisation year and three years of slow drift with a working acquisition engine. It is set out below as the Consumer should model it, with the operational drivers first and the line items second, and the forecast inputs file carries the same numbers.

Operational drivers.

Tungsten and BETEK. Western APT falls from its August 2026 peak above USD 3,100 per MTU toward the USD 1,500 to 2,000 range during H1 2027 as Sangdong ramps and the Western-Chinese gap closes from the Chinese side, while Chinese raw-material export restrictions persist and the US defence rule from January 2027 keeps a traceable-supply premium in Western feedstock. BETEK's selling prices follow with a lag of one to two quarters; the cost of goods follows with a lag of two to three quarters, so the margin squeeze is concentrated in Q4 2026 and H1 2027. BETEK retains about half of its 2026 price gain in FY27 and about a third from FY28, and about a third of the volume it took from retreating competitors. Materials Solutions revenue EUR 757m in FY26 (+37%), EUR 660m in FY27 (-13%), then +3 to 4% a year; segment adjusted EBITA margin 18% in FY26, 10% in FY27, 9.5% in FY28, 9.5 to 10% thereafter. A EUR 5m inventory allowance in Q4 2026 is in the base; a larger one is the main downside.

Engineering. Organic volume of 3 to 4% a year on backlog conversion (the EUR 430m record backlog with multi-year US plant projects delivering in 2027 and 2028), short-cycle demand flat in FY26 and FY27 and recovering with German capacity utilisation from 2028; price flat to slightly negative. Revenue EUR 629m in FY26 (+8%, with 4.7 points inorganic), EUR 654m in FY27 (+4%), then +4 to 5%. Margin 8.5% in FY26 (H1 5.6%, H2 about 11.5% on normal Q4 seasonality), 9.0% in FY27, 9.5% in FY28, 9.5 to 10% thereafter; the 8.5 to 10.5% band is reached and not exceeded.

Infrastructure. Digital-network and grid demand (fibre entry systems, grid sealing, power distribution) grows at double digits in orders and high single digits in revenue; residential construction stays depressed through 2027 and recovers slowly; the EUR 500bn German fund converts to orders from 2027 and revenue from H2 2027. Revenue EUR 626m in FY26 (+5%), EUR 648m in FY27 (+3.5%), then +4 to 5%. Margin 11.3% in FY26 and FY27, 11.5% thereafter, inside the 10 to 12% band.

Acquisitions. One or two bolt-ons in Q4 2026 (EUR 10 to 15m); EUR 55 to 60m of deals in FY27 as working capital releases; EUR 75m, 85m and 95m in FY28 to FY30 at entry multiples drifting from 6x toward 7x EV/EBIT; acquired revenue of about 1% in FY27 and about 3% a year from FY28; acquired EBITA of EUR 12 to 16m a year from FY28. Disposals of 0.3 to 1% of sales a year. The 2030 inorganic bridge of EUR 600m of revenue is reached about half-way.

Costs and holding. Personnel cost ratio rises from 31.9% (FY25) to about 30% in FY26 on price-led revenue and back to about 32.5 to 33% in FY27, then drifts down 20 basis points a year on productivity; materials ratio falls as tungsten deflates; holding costs EUR 22 to 25m. Capex on a cash basis 2.5 to 2.8% of sales; leases EUR 22 to 26m a year.

Working capital. FactSet-definition net working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables) of EUR 707m (37.9% of trailing revenue) at June 2026 falls to about EUR 680m (34%) at end-2026, EUR 600m (30.5%) at end-2027, then 29%, 28.5% and 28% as the cash conversion cycle shortens from 203 days toward 180; the pre-shock level was 30 to 33%.

Against consensus and guidance. FY26: revenue at the guidance midpoint and consensus; adjusted EBITA at the guidance midpoint; EPS 7% below the consensus median on tax and interest; free cash flow at the low end of "at least break-even". FY28: revenue in line, EBITDA 5% below, EPS 10% below. Against the 2030 targets: revenue EUR 2.28bn against about EUR 3bn, adjusted EBITA EUR 221m against above EUR 330m, margin 9.7% against 11%, EPS EUR 4.64 after PPA against above EUR 4.50.

What would move the base case, and by how much. Each 10% of BETEK's 2026 price retained or lost in FY27 beyond the half I assume: about EUR 10m of adjusted EBITA and EUR 0.27 of EPS. An inventory write-down of EUR 20m rather than EUR 5m: EUR 0.40 of FY26 or FY27 EPS, no cash effect. Engineering at 8% rather than 9% in FY27: EUR 6.5m and EUR 0.17. Tax at 31% rather than 34%: EUR 0.15 of FY27 EPS. Working capital at 33% rather than 30.5% of revenue at end-2027: EUR 50m less free cash flow in FY27. Acquisitions at the 2030 plan's EUR 100m a year from FY27: about EUR 0.15 of EPS a year cumulative from FY28. A German capex and construction recovery broad enough to lift Engineering to 10.5% and Infrastructure to 12% by FY28: about EUR 16m and EUR 0.45 of EPS, which is the bull case for the drift years.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue. FY26 +15.5%: H1 actual +15.4%, H2 about +16% on Materials price still elevated in Q3 and fading in Q4, Engineering backlog conversion with its normal Q4 peak, Infrastructure digital-network demand, and about 2 points inorganic (PRO VIDEO, AMIRA, prior-year add-ons). FY27 -2.0%: Materials Solutions -13% as BETEK gives back about half of its 2026 price gain and about half of the volume taken from retreating competitors, with the hardest comparison in Q2 2027 against the Q2 2026 peak; Engineering +4% on the EUR 430m backlog (US plant projects delivering in 2027 and 2028); Infrastructure +3.5% as grid and fibre demand offsets residential weakness and the EUR 500bn German fund converts to revenue from H2 2027; about 1 point inorganic from deals done in Q4 2026 and H1 2027. FY28 to FY30 +5 to 5.5%: organic volume 2 to 3% (German capacity utilisation and construction recovering from 2028, price 1 to 2% in Infrastructure and the non-tungsten Materials units, flat in Engineering) plus 2 to 3% acquired revenue from EUR 75 to 95m a year of acquisitions at 5.5 to 7x EV/EBIT.

Margin. The FY26 to FY27 move is price leverage in reverse at BETEK (feedstock bought at USD 2,000 to 3,100 per MTU in H1 2026 processed through H2 2026 and H1 2027 under a nine-month industry cost lag, while selling prices adjusted at short intervals fall with Western APT as Chinese domestic prices have already collapsed and Sangdong supply arrives), plus about EUR 17 to 20m of German wage inflation on a semi-fixed labour base while revenue falls, only half offset by productivity. From FY28 the margin drifts up 20 to 30 basis points a year on Engineering utilisation, acquired mix (targets with EBIT margins above 10%), lean and ERP programmes and disposals of underperformers; the 2030 target of 11% is the ceiling, not the base.

Depreciation (excluding PPA): EUR 74m in FY25, rising with the Grand Rapids and Serbian facilities, acquisitions and right-of-use assets. PPA amortisation, which the model's D&A excludes, is EUR 21.5m in FY26, 22m in FY27, 24m, 25m and 26m thereafter; the brokers deduct it, so the model's EPS runs EUR 0.58 to 0.74 above the brokers' basis.

Net financial items: net interest of about EUR 25m in FY25 on average net debt of about EUR 580m at an all-in cost of about 4.3 to 4.5% (promissory notes, swapped bank debt at 2.49% on EUR 185m, lease interest), rising to about EUR 32m in FY26 on the peak working-capital funding and falling to EUR 27 to 28m as net debt falls and then re-levers with acquisitions; includes put-option revaluations on EUR 37.8m of minority commitments and other financial items.

Tax: structural rate of 33 to 34% for a holding of limited partnerships whose trade-tax losses at loss-making subsidiaries cannot be offset (EUR 5.98m of FY25 tax expense); H1 2026 ran at about 36%; the FY25 rate of 28.7% included an EUR 8.36m one-off credit. From FY28 the German corporate income tax rate falls one point a year, taking about a point a year off the group rate.

Dividend: 40 to 50% payout policy; FY26 at the low end of policy on windfall earnings with a stretched balance sheet (EUR 1.90 on EPS of about EUR 4.85 after PPA), FY27 EUR 1.50 as earnings fall, then rising with earnings.

Share count: 24.9m shares outstanding (25.8m issued less about 3.5% treasury); no change other than buybacks; no buybacks in FY26 to FY28 while working capital and acquisitions absorb cash and the share trades at the top of its own multiple range; a token EUR 10m a year in FY29 and FY30.

Capital spending (cash, fixed assets and intangibles): EUR 48m in FY25 on FactSet's cash basis against the company's EUR 60.6m "investment in property and equipment" (which I read as including right-of-use additions) and consensus of EUR 74 to 88m (which appears to be on that broader basis); 2.5 to 2.8% of sales on the cash basis.

Working capital (FactSet definition: receivables plus inventories less payables, as a percentage of trailing revenue; the company's own narrower definition was EUR 627m at June 2026 against FactSet's EUR 707m): 37.9% at June 2026, 34% at end-2026 as some inventory releases in H2, 30.5% at end-2027 as tungsten inventory values and receivables deflate (a release of about EUR 80m in FY27), then a slow structural improvement from 203 days of cash conversion cycle toward 180 days. The pre-shock level was 30 to 33%.

Leases: about EUR 22m a year on EUR 88m of lease liabilities, rising with the US facility and acquisitions; outside both the company's and FactSet's free cash flow definitions.

Acquisitions less disposals: H1 2026 EUR 16.6m plus one or two bolt-ons in Q4 2026 (the company said on 5 October 2026 it expects further acquisitions before year-end) and minority buy-outs, less MEWESTA proceeds; EUR 55 to 60m in FY27 as working capital releases and the policy thresholds (net debt to EBITDA below 2.5x, equity ratio toward 40%) are met; EUR 75m, 85m and 95m in FY28 to FY30, below the 2030 plan's EUR 100m-plus a year; disposals of 0.3 to 1% of sales a year under the 2025 policy.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

FY26 guidance (29 July 2026): revenue EUR 1.90 to 2.10bn; adjusted EBITA EUR 220 to 260m (margin 11 to 13%); Engineering margin 8.5 to 10.5%; Infrastructure 10 to 12%; Materials Solutions 15 to 17%; free cash flow at least break-even. My inputs: revenue EUR 2,004m (midpoint); adjusted EBITA EUR 238m (midpoint; implied H2 of EUR 114.5m against H1 123.5m and H2 2025 91.7m); Engineering 8.5% (low end), Infrastructure 11.3% (middle), Materials Solutions 18% (above the range, because H1 is banked and my H2 at about 14% still averages above 17%; a full-year 16% would need an H2 margin near 10%, which would take the group to about EUR 225m); free cash flow on the company's definition slightly positive, consistent with "at least break-even". No guidance exists beyond FY26 other than the 2030 ambition (revenue about EUR 3bn, adjusted EBITA above EUR 330m, margin 11%, EPS above EUR 4.50, EUR 500m of acquisitions adding EUR 600m of revenue); my FY30 is revenue EUR 2.28bn, adjusted EBITA EUR 221m, margin 9.7%, EPS EUR 4.64 after PPA and EUR 5.38 before, acquisitions of about EUR 340m cumulative over FY26 to FY30. I expect initial FY27 guidance, set in March 2027, to be an adjusted EBITA margin range of about 8 to 10% and a free cash flow target well above FY26, which my inputs sit inside.

Against consensus (FactSet median, 22 October 2026): FY26 revenue 2,016 (mine 2,004), EBITDA 315 (mine 314), EPS 5.21 (mine 4.85 after PPA), free cash flow 32 on FactSet's definition (mine about 28 before leases); FY27 revenue 2,043 (mine 1,962), EBITDA 292 (mine 255; the two brokers updated since the October conference, Metzler and NuWays, are at 272 and 263), EPS 3.94 (mine 3.22; Metzler 3.65, NuWays 3.63), free cash flow 131 (mine about 210 before leases, because consensus operating cash flow of 206 embeds no working-capital release); FY28 revenue 2,158 (mine 2,070), EBITDA 290 (mine 276), EPS 4.15 (mine 3.73).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

The company guides and reports adjusted EBITA (EBIT before amortisation of acquired intangibles and impairments; FY25 EUR 147.8m against reported EBIT of EUR 127.0m, a gap of EUR 20.8m of PPA amortisation). Brokers' EBITDA, the basis of FactSet consensus, is adjusted EBITA plus depreciation excluding PPA (FY25: 147.8 + about 74 = 222, the FactSet brokers'-basis actual); it is not FactSet's operating EBITDA history line (EUR 230m in FY25), which sits on a third basis and should not be used for growth to consensus. Brokers' EBIT (FY26 consensus 215) is adjusted EBITA less PPA amortisation and a small amount of other items. Brokers' EPS is after PPA amortisation and before one-offs (FY25 EUR 2.96 against reported basic EUR 2.77; the EUR 0.19 gap is one-offs, not PPA). The forecast-inputs form takes D&A excluding PPA, so the model's EBIT equals adjusted EBITA and the model's EPS is before PPA amortisation; to compare the model's EPS with consensus, deduct PPA amortisation after tax at the model's rate (EUR 21.5m at 34% is EUR 0.57 per share in FY26; EUR 22m is EUR 0.58 in FY27; EUR 24m at 33% is EUR 0.64 in FY28; EUR 25m at 32% is EUR 0.69 in FY29; EUR 26m at 31% is EUR 0.74 in FY30). Net financial debt on the company's definition excludes lease liabilities (EUR 665.8m at June 2026); FactSet's debt includes them (EUR 88m). The company's free cash flow definition (FY25 EUR 124m) differs from FactSet's operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex (FY25 EUR 72m); my cross-check free cash flow is operating cash flow after tax and interest, less capex including intangibles, less lease payments, before acquisitions, dividends and buybacks.

Earnings per share above is on the model's basis (before PPA amortisation, after tax at the rates in Part 1, after minorities of EUR 1.2m, 1.5m, 2.0m, 2.0m and 2.5m, on 24.9m shares falling to about 24.4m by FY30). On the brokers' basis, after PPA amortisation, the same years are EUR 4.85, 3.22, 3.73, 4.17 and 4.64. Free cash flow is after lease payments and before acquisitions, dividends and buybacks; on FactSet's definition (before leases and intangibles) it is about EUR 28m, 211m, 142m, 137m and 149m. The FY27 figure includes a working-capital release of about EUR 80m that depends on tungsten prices falling as I expect; if Western prices hold at their 2026 level through 2027, the release is about EUR 30m and earnings per share is about EUR 0.50 higher.

Research requests for the next set

Company: INDUS Holding AG (INH-DE), a German permanent-ownership industrial holding listed on the SDAX, owning 43 small and medium-sized industrial technology companies in three segments of roughly equal size: Engineering (customised plant, automation, sensors, measurement and media technology), Infrastructure (cable entry and grid hardware, building technology, formwork and joint systems, HVAC) and Materials Solutions (carbide-tipped wear tools through subsidiary BETEK, metal processing, medical and orthopaedic products, micro-optics)

Request: T-D1

Peers: Sandvik AB (SAND-SE; its Machining business area is the integrated carbide-tool and tungsten-powder producer whose price-cost-neutral policy and stated pricing and cost lags are the industry reference for what a toolmaker keeps when tungsten normalises); Question: What is the structural effective tax rate of INDUS Holding AG, given its disclosure that trade tax is assessed at the level of individual limited partnerships so that losses at loss-making subsidiaries cannot be offset (EUR 5.98m of additional tax expense in FY2025, annual report 2025), the EUR 8.36m one-off credit on the SMA sale in FY2025, reported rates of 28.7% to 43.1% since FY2023, and the German corporate income tax rate reduction legislated in 2025 to take effect from 2028; what rate do each of the covering brokers assume for FY2026 to FY2028, what deferred-tax revaluation effect the 2028 rate cut will have on INDUS's reported earnings and when, and how Gesco SE's and other German industrial holdings' tax rates have behaved under the same structure through industrial downturns?

Request: OWN-5

Question: What evidence exists on the pace, pricing and sourcing of INDUS Holding AG's acquisition programme since the EMPOWERING MITTELSTAND strategy was announced in March 2025 (EUR 500m by 2030, first-tier targets with at least EUR 4m of adjusted EBITA, geography widened to Benelux, Italy, Spain, Poland and Scandinavia): the deals completed (PRO VIDEO, AMIRA, METFAB, SUNBELT, Electro Trading, Kettler, TRIGOSYS, the DECKMA and MESUTRONIC minority buy-outs) with their disclosed sizes and multiples, acquired revenue and EBITA delivered against the company's own targets, the pipeline and timing management has described since the 5 October 2026 statement that further acquisitions are expected before year-end, the entry multiples now being paid in German succession transactions by Lifco, Indutrade, Sdiptech, Addtech, family offices and private equity in 2025 to 2026, and what German SME advisers and sellers say about INDUS's non-exit promise after the MEWESTA disposal?

Request: OWN-6

Question: Across the stock market narrative on INDUS Holding AG since the 29 July 2026 guidance raise, what do the sell-side notes (NuWays, Metzler, Oddo BHF, Pareto, Parmantier, mwb, Warburg/MP Capital Markets), the conference feedback of October 2026, German small-cap investor forums and Twitter/X say about how the Q3 2026 report on 12 November 2026 will be judged: which figures (Materials Solutions quarterly margin, working capital and free cash flow, Engineering margin, order intake, acquisition announcements, any inventory valuation allowance) are named as the tests, what thresholds or expectations are stated for each, how the share price has reacted to each of the last eight INDUS earnings releases relative to the SDAX and to the surprise against consensus, and how the share price and 24-month-forward P/E have moved against Gesco, Aalberts, TKH, Lifco, Indutrade, Storskogen and Sdiptech on a rolling six-month basis since 2024?
